### Cross-layer and shared KV: architectures that reduce the KV cache by construction rather than by quantizing it — CLA (share KV across adjacent layers), YOCO (cache once, cross-decode), LCKV (all queri

**State of the art.** Three distinct SOTAs, because the slice splits by what you are allowed to do to the model.

1. PRETRAIN-FROM-SCRATCH (frontier): DeepSeek-V4 (arXiv:2606.19348, Apr 2026). ~2% of a BF16 GQA-8 cache at 1M context, i.e. ~50x, via interleaved Compressed Sparse Attention (4x sequence compression + sparse top-k) and Heavily Compressed Attention (128x sequence compression + dense), with FP8 content / BF16 RoPE mixed storage supplying ~2x of that. Runner-up and more reproducible: Kimi Linear (arXiv:2510.26692), 3:1 KDA-to-MLA, 75% KV reduction and 6x decode at 1M while beating full MLA.

2. FINE-TUNE-A-CHECKPOINT (retrofit): TransMLA (arXiv:2502.07864, NeurIPS 2025 Spotlight). 68.75% KV compression training-free at 1.65% drop on LLaMA-2-7B; 93% with 6B tokens of fine-tuning; 10.6x vLLM speedup. MHA2MLA (arXiv:2502.14837) is the competing number at 92.19% reduction / 1% LongBench drop using 0.6-1% of pretraining tokens. The two papers disagree sharply about each other's degradation.

3. TRAINING-FREE (the only tier a 24 GB solo setup can enter): xKV (arXiv:2503.18893, ICML 2026). Up to 8x cross-layer compression at ≤3% accuracy loss on Llama-3.1 / Qwen2.5, 6.8x higher compression than MiniCache with +2.7% accuracy, and — decisively — a further 3-3.5x on DeepSeek-V2-Coder-Lite's already-compressed MLA latent with no degradation. Distant second: KVSharer at 30% reduction, MiniCache at 1.53x alone / 5.02x with 4-bit.

The number that most constrains the researcher's positioning: for PURE cross-layer sharing (CLA/YOCO/LCKV), the defensible figure is 2x essentially free, established at only 110M and 1.1B parameters on 1.7B training tokens (arXiv:2410.14442). Everything larger than 2x in this slice comes from low-rank latents, sequence-dimension compression, or linear-attention layers — not from layer sharing per se.

**Collision risk.** Being deliberately aggressive. Four items threaten a novelty claim; several more constrain framing.

TIER 1 — SERIOUS, ACT ON THESE

1. RippleKV (arXiv:2608.08684, Aug 2026) — the closest methodological collision anywhere in this slice, published weeks ago. It injects norm-adaptive perturbations into one layer's cache at a time WITH NO QUANTIZER INVOLVED, measures induced output KL on a small calibration set, and derives a model-specific sensitivity profile that is explicitly non-monotonic in depth. Its motivating critique is nearly verbatim the researcher's: 'Existing methods rely on proxies such as layer depth, attention statistics, or representation change. These proxies do not measure how perturbations at each layer propagate to the output.' The primitive — synthetic-noise calibration → per-model response curve → replace proxies — is now published. SURVIVING DIFFERENTIATORS, and they are real: RippleKV perturbs VALUES only (the researcher does keys); measures KL at the output (not end-to-end perplexity); produces a RELATIVE ranking used to allocate token budgets, not an ABSOLUTE calibrated law that predicts a configuration's true damage; has no held-out extrapolation to unseen bit-widths, no catastrophe gate, no cross-model atlas, and no coverage of the ×1000 catastrophic regime. The researcher must cite it, and must lead with absolute calibrated prediction + catastrophe gating rather than with 'noise injection reveals sensitivity', which is now prior art.

2. 'Quantization Dominates Rank Reduction for KV-Cache Compression' (arXiv:2604.11501) — dangerous on two fronts. First, it reports 'Attention KL is monotonically correlated with PPL (r=0.99)' across five models (124M-14B, MHA and GQA). A score-space-metric-predicts-perplexity-damage result is therefore in print, at a correlation comparable to the researcher's R²=0.971. Second, it supplies a mechanism and a bound: under the softmax Fisher metric, projection damage exceeds quantization damage by 3×2^{2b} per direction, with routing-flip rates (4.6% for rank-32 vs 0.03% for INT4) as the causal chain. It also states that below 3 bits quantization noise 'exceeds typical gaps, triggering the same instability (INT2: 73-138 PPL)' — reaching into the catastrophic regime the researcher claims as novel. Differentiators: their r=0.99 is a within-experiment correlation used to explain one comparison, not a calibrated per-model curve evaluated at arbitrary configurations; they have no held-out bit-width prediction, no 13-model law, no misses/gate analysis. But the sentence 'score-space error predicts perplexity damage' can no longer be presented as new.

3. xKV (arXiv:2503.18893, ICML 2026) — collides with the atlas's headline negative result. xKV's stated motivation is that prior cross-layer methods 'rely on assumptions of high per-token cosine similarity across layers which generally does not hold in practice,' and it demonstrates the consequence (MiniCache degrades at 1.3x while CKA-guided xKV works at 8x). 'Per-vector cosine similarity is the wrong signal, here is a better geometric one' is published, in the cross-layer setting, with a replacement metric (Centered Kernel Alignment). The researcher's Pearson −0.003 in the QUANTIZATION setting is still distinct and is a stronger, cleaner statement — but it should be framed as confirming and extending a known failure of cosine, not discovering it.

4. Block-GTQ / 'RoPE-Aware Bit Allocation for KV-Cache Quantization' (arXiv:2606.24033) — this one builds directly on the researcher's own algorithmic lineage. It takes TurboQuant-MSE as its base quantizer, uses its 4^{-b} error law, and adds a RoPE-block energy allocator. It runs a ten-model diagnostic panel spanning Qwen, Llama, DeepSeek, Mistral, GLM plus an MLA-based DeepSeek model; it evaluates in score space (per-layer RoPE-logit MAE, softmax KL vs fp16, top-10 attended-token overlap); and it reports uniform TQ-MSE COLLAPSING to 0.0/0.0 on AIME at K3V2 where Block-GTQ scores 51.7/3

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- FusedKV (arXiv:2512.03870) concedes the whole subfield underperforms: 'Although Cross-layer KV Cache sharing (e.g., YOCO, CLA) offers a path to mitigate KV Cache bottleneck, it typically underperforms within-layer methods like GQA.' Their diagnosis — top-layer values come mostly from the bottom layer while top-layer keys draw from bottom AND middle — is an asymmetry established only on 332M-4B models and never connected to why keys and values also behave asymmetrically under quantization.
- LCKV (arXiv:2405.10637) states as future work: 'designing more efficient training approaches, developing large-batch-friendly kernels, and verifying our method on larger and more complex LLMs.' Two years on, the verification-at-scale item is still open — the entire layer-sharing evidence base tops out around 3B.
- The systematic study (arXiv:2410.14442) confines all nine configurations to 110M and 1.1B parameters trained on MiniPile (1.7B tokens), and concludes only that beyond 2x you must accept 'additional training cost and prefilling latency.' There is no per-model prediction of where the 2x cliff actually sits, and no evidence the ranking of configurations survives to 7B+.
- TransMLA's NeurIPS version lists its limitations explicitly: 'error accumulation becomes increasingly severe for longer texts, making recovery more challenging'; 'potential issues may arise during training aimed at enhancing recovery performance, including subtle performance degradation and hallucinations'; 'it is worth exploring more advanced mathematical approaches that can better reconcile the norm disparity between keys and values... ultimately enabling a truly training-free conversion process'; 'MLA is less compatible with tensor parallelism (TP) than GQA'; and, as an outright invitation, 'TransMLA should be integrated with pruning, quantization, token selection, and other optimization techniques to fully explore the upper limits of inference acceleration.'
- MHA2MLA (arXiv:2502.14837) asserts that 'MHA2MLA's latent space design remains orthogonal to numerical precision reduction, enabling compound efficiency gains without destructive interference' — a claim about how architectural and numerical damage compose, supported by roughly four measured cells. How the two damages actually combine is unquantified anywhere in the slice.
- 'Through the Bottleneck' (arXiv:2607.23054) disclaims its own scope: findings are 'from a single 114M-parameter model trained on a narrow-domain corpus'; 'confirming this as a general property of MLA, rather than an artifact of this model or dataset, requires replication across seeds, scales, and domains.' Its stated priorities are matched MHA baselines under identical conditions, multi-seed replication, and 'testing heterogeneous rank allocation empirically, ideally at production scale.' It also leaves as an untested conjecture that content-only c_KV 'may be more amenable to semantic-aware compression... than standard K/V caches, which interleave content and position.'
- xKV (arXiv:2503.18893) reports without explanation that MiniCache and single-layer SVD 'fail to preserve accuracy on the MLA architecture even at substantially lower compression rates' while xKV succeeds. Why MLA latents resist naive post-hoc compression but tolerate cross-layer factorization is unresolved, and is a mechanism question.
- SnapMLA (arXiv:2602.10718) characterizes MLA's content/RoPE quantization-sensitivity divergence only at FP8 and only on LongCat-class models. Nobody has measured MLA latents at 2-4 bits, where the researcher's catastrophic regime lives, and the paper concedes that 'in high-concurrency inference scenarios, architectural compression alone is insufficient.'
- MiniCache (arXiv:2405.14366) lists as future work 'enhancing the compression ratio by cross-multiple-layer merging, developing advanced merging algorithms such as Spherical Cubic Interpolation, and further optimizing memory usage' — i.e. its 1.53x standalone ratio is acknowledged as leaving room.
- The rate-distortion survey (arXiv:2607.08032) names the field-level gap: 'while compression is measured carefully on single-turn long context, the repeated compaction that agents actually perform is almost never measured, and no benchmark holds one budget axis across all the layers at once.' It also finds that across every layer of the stack the retention signal is attention magnitude or recency and 'it fails in the same way everywhere.'
- Stochastic KV Routing (arXiv:2604.22782) frames the standing obstacle for post-hoc cross-layer sharing: 'Post-hoc approaches were also proposed to enable cache sharing across layers, but sharing is done only to a limited extent as models are trained with layer-specific Keys and Values.' Whether a checkpoint's tolerance for cross-layer sharing is predictable from measurable properties of that checkpoint is unaddressed.
- KVDiagnosis (arXiv:2608.09412) explicitly excludes the architectural tier from its benchmark: 'Architectural KV-head reduction, paging, offloading, scheduling, and cross-request reuse are related mechanisms rather than benchmark subjects.' No diagnostic benchmark currently covers architectural KV reduction at all.

**Key works.**

- `arXiv:2405.12981 (NeurIPS 2024)` (2024-05-21) **Reducing Transformer Key-Value Cache Size with Cross-Layer Attention (CLA)** — Shares K/V activations across adjacent layers; only a subset of layers compute KV projections. Sharing factor 2 (CLA2) gives exactly 2x KV reduction. Trained 1B and 3B models from scratch. Combined with MQA, CLA2 yields 2x reduction at <1% perplexity change; CLA3/CLA4 Pareto-dominate plain MQA but a *Relevance:* The canonical layer-sharing baseline and the origin of the '2x is free' number. Requires pretraining from scratch — out of reach on one 4090 — so it is context, not a build target. Its measured perplexity-vs-KV-budget curves at 1B/3B are, however, exactly the kind of data a damage law should be able
- `arXiv:2405.04434` (2024-05-07) **DeepSeek-V2 (Multi-head Latent Attention, MLA)** — Caches a single low-rank latent c_KV per token per layer instead of per-head K and V, with a decoupled-RoPE side channel because RoPE does not commute with the up-projection. KV cache = (d_c + d_h^R)·l ≈ 4.5·d_h·l, equivalent to GQA with 2.25 groups. Reports 93.3% KV reduction vs DeepSeek-67B and qu *Relevance:* The single most important architecture in the slice, and the source of the strongest evidence that architecture does NOT obviate quantization: DeepSeek explicitly states they additionally quantize the deployed MLA cache to ~6 bits average. Also note MLA splits the cache into a content part and a RoP
- `arXiv:2405.05254 (NeurIPS 2024 oral)` (2024-05-08) **You Only Cache Once: Decoder-Decoder Architectures (YOCO)** — Self-decoder (efficient/sliding-window attention) produces one global KV cache; a stacked cross-decoder reuses it via cross-attention. Cache is O(N + CL) instead of O(NL), roughly an L-fold saving (~80x claimed for 65B). Also enables prefill early-exit, cutting 512K prefill from 180s to <6s. *Relevance:* The most aggressive pure layer-sharing design and the upper bound of what 'share the cache' can buy. Requires pretraining. Its real contribution to the researcher's framing is that YOCO's saving comes from making most layers not-global at all — the same trick Gemma-3 and the hybrids industrialized.
- `arXiv:2405.10637 (ACL 2024)` (2024-05-17) **Layer-Condensed KV Cache (LCKV)** — Pairs queries of ALL layers with keys/values of only the top layer, plus a few 'warmup' layers (top w/2 and bottom w/2) kept standard to recover quality. Needs an approximate iterative training scheme because of the cyclic dependency. Reports up to 26x throughput and 32x larger batches on 1B-30B Lla *Relevance:* The extreme end of layer sharing. Its warmup-layer finding — that the first and last layers cannot be shared without a large drop — is an architectural echo of the researcher's own boundary-layer k_proj-bias mechanism. Authors admit they never verified it on larger/more complex LLMs.
- `arXiv:2410.14442 (NAACL 2025 Short)` (2024-10-18) **A Systematic Study of Cross-Layer KV Sharing for Efficient LLM Inference** — Unifies LCKV, YOCO and CLA as special cases of one framework (partitioning ∈ {pizza, sandwich, lasagna} × target-layer position ∈ {bottom, top, middle}); LCKV = sandwich-top, YOCO = pizza-bottom, CLA = lasagna-bottom. Trains all 9 configurations at 110M and 1.1B from scratch on MiniPile (1.7B tokens *Relevance:* The most honest and most quantitative answer in the slice to 'how much do layer-sharing architectures solve outright' — the answer is 2x, cleanly, and then it gets expensive. Crucially the whole study is at 110M and 1.1B on 1.7B tokens. Nobody has established that these conclusions survive to 7-14B,
- `arXiv:2410.18517` (2024-10-24) **KVSharer: Efficient Inference via Layer-Wise Dissimilar KV Cache Sharing** — Training-free, plug-and-play layer-wise KV sharing on already-trained LLMs. Counterintuitive core claim: sharing DISSIMILAR layers' KV caches preserves quality better than sharing similar ones. Greedy search over layer pairs, accepting a pair when cosine similarity of the mean final-layer hidden sta *Relevance:* The only training-free member of the layer-sharing family and therefore the one thing in this slice the researcher can actually run on a 4090 today. Note its acceptance criterion is a cheap output-space cosine proxy chosen without validation — precisely the class of proxy the researcher's atlas show
- `arXiv:2405.14366 (NeurIPS 2024)` (2024-05-23) **MiniCache: KV Cache Compression in Depth Dimension** — Training-free cross-layer merging from the midpoint of the model onward. Disentangles KV states into magnitude and direction, SLERPs the directions while preserving per-token norms, and retains 'unmergeable' outlier token pairs (selected by angular distance) separately. 1.53x alone on LLaMA-2-7B; 5. *Relevance:* Two things matter here. First, the magnitude/direction split plus explicit outlier retention is structurally the same move as the researcher's mean-removal + residual-sign design, arrived at independently in the cross-layer setting. Second, MiniCache is the clearest published demonstration that cros
- `arXiv:2503.18893 (ICML 2026)` (2025-03-24 (v2 2026-05-27)) **xKV: Cross-Layer KV-Cache Compression via Aligned Singular Vector Extraction** — Training-free, post-training cross-layer SVD: groups W adjacent layers, horizontally concatenates their caches, and factorizes into a shared token basis A plus per-layer reconstruction matrices B_l. Justified by Centered Kernel Alignment showing dominant singular vectors are well aligned across laye *Relevance:* The most important single paper in this slice for the researcher. (a) It is the current training-free SOTA and beats the whole trained layer-sharing family without touching weights. (b) It explicitly attacks per-token cosine similarity as the wrong cross-layer signal — the researcher's headline nega
- `arXiv:2502.07864 (NeurIPS 2025 Spotlight)` (2025-02-11) **TransMLA: Migrating GQA Models to MLA with Full DeepSeek Compatibility** — Proves MLA strictly subsumes GQA in expressive power at equal KV budget, then converts any GQA checkpoint (LLaMA, Qwen, Gemma, Mistral/Mixtral) into DeepSeek-format MLA. Uses RoRoPE + FreqFold to concentrate positional signal into one head, drops RoPE elsewhere, and Balanced-KV-norm joint PCA. 68.75 *Relevance:* The main retrofit path, and a 68.75% training-free operating point the researcher could in principle reproduce on a small model. Note the norm-disparity-between-keys-and-values problem that Balanced-KV solves is a first-moment/scale pathology of exactly the type the researcher has already diagnosed 
- `arXiv:2502.14837 (ACL 2025 Long)` (2025-02-20) **MHA2MLA: Towards Economical Inference — Enabling DeepSeek's MLA in Any Transformer-based LLM** — First data-efficient MHA/GQA→MLA fine-tuning: contribution-aware partial-RoPE (keep top-k dimensions by attention-score impact, NoPE the rest) plus joint SVD of W_k/W_v on the NoPE subspace. Recovers with 0.6-1% of pretraining tokens. Llama2-7B: 92.19% KV reduction at 1% LongBench drop. Explicitly s *Relevance:* The single most quantitative published claim about architecture×quantization composition, and it is thin: a handful of cells asserting 'MLA's latent space remains orthogonal to numerical precision reduction, enabling compound efficiency gains without destructive interference.' That is an empirical c
- `arXiv:2503.19786` (2025-03-25) **Gemma 3 Technical Report (5:1 local/global interleaving)** — Interleaves 5 sliding-window (1024-token) local attention layers per 1 global layer, so only 1/6 of layers hold full-context KV. Reports KV memory overhead at 32K context falling from 60% (global-only) to <15%. Ablations: local:global ratio has 'minimal impact on perplexity, even with 7-to-1'; slidi *Relevance:* The shipped, consumer-hardware-relevant form of layer heterogeneity, and it is directly runnable on a 4090 (Gemma-3 4B/12B). It creates a naturally heterogeneous cache — a few global layers carrying essentially all the long-range information — which is precisely the setting where a worst-layer score
- `arXiv:2510.26692` (2025-10-30) **Kimi Linear: hybrid Kimi Delta Attention + MLA** — 48B-total / 3B-activated MoE, layerwise 3:1 ratio of Kimi Delta Attention (a channel-wise-gated Gated DeltaNet variant with fixed-size recurrent state, no growing KV) to full MLA layers. Reports up to 75% KV cache reduction and up to 6x decoding throughput at 1M context while outperforming a full-ML *Relevance:* State of the art for architectural KV reduction in an open checkpoint, and the clearest statement that the field's answer is now 'make most layers not cache at all.' At 48B it is out of comfortable 4090 range, but it defines the ceiling the researcher's quantization results should be positioned agai

---

### Long-context evaluation: which benchmarks (RULER, LongBench/v2, NIAH, InfiniteBench, HELMET, and their 2025-26 successors) are treated as trustworthy in Aug 2026, which have been publicly criticized, 

**State of the art.** As of Aug 2026 there is no single trusted long-context benchmark; the community has settled on a layered protocol, and the classic suites have been visibly demoted.

TRUSTED (in rough order of standing):
1. HELMET (arXiv:2410.02694, ICLR 2025) + LongProc (arXiv:2501.05414, COLM 2025) — the holistic reference pair. HELMET's defining numbers: 59 LCLMs, 7 application categories to 128K, and the two results everyone quotes — NIAH is saturated yet does NOT predict downstream performance, and HELMET's own categories have LOW mutual correlation, so no single task substitutes for the suite. Its recommendation is that RAG tasks are the best fast proxy.
2. LongBench v2 (arXiv:2412.15204) and LongBench Pro (arXiv:2601.02872, Jan 2026) — the realistic-reasoning references. LongBench v2's defining number: human experts 53.7% under a 15-minute limit vs 50.1% for the best directly-answering model and 57.7% for o1-preview. LongBench Pro: 1,500 samples, 46 models, 8k-256k, and the finding that effective context is typically far shorter than claimed context.
3. RULER (arXiv:2404.06654, COLM 2024) — retained as a DIAGNOSTIC stress test, not a capability score. Defining number: of 17 models all claiming >=32K, only about half maintain satisfactory performance at 32K. It is the benchmark NVIDIA's kvpress leaderboard reports on, which makes it the de facto KV-compression standard.
4. NoLiMa (arXiv:2502.05167, ICML 2025) and HaystackCraft (arXiv:2510.07414) — the credible NIAH successors. NoLiMa's defining number: at 32K, 11 of 13 models fall below 50% of their short-context baseline; GPT-4o 99.3% -> 69.7%.

CRITICIZED / DEMOTED:
- Vanilla NIAH: saturated, measures lexical retrieval only, no peer-reviewed paper, does not predict downstream (HELMET; Chroma Context Rot, Jul 2025).
- LongBench v1: average length ~6.7K words, ROUGE/F1 metrics that LongBench v2's own authors call 'known to be unreliable', no position control, contamination exposure (LV-Eval arXiv:2402.05136).
- InfiniteBench: narrow domain coverage and noisy metrics.
- The strongest single data point on 2026 standing: arXiv:2605.23170 audited four flagship 2026 releases (DeepSeek-V4-Pro, MiMo-V2.5-Pro, Kimi-K2.6, GLM-5.1) and found 0 of 28 main-result-table cells reporting NIAH/RULER/LongBench-family benchmarks, versus 20 of 28 reporting agentic/coding benchmarks. The classic long-context suites now live in appendices.

FOR KV-CACHE COMPRESSION SPECIFICALLY, the expected protocol in 2026 is: LongBench (legacy, still 

**Collision risk.** Ranked, most dangerous first. Nothing I found builds a noise-injection-calibrated damage law for KV caches — that core idea appears clear. But four works occupy adjacent ground closely enough to threaten specific claims, and one threatens the framing outright.

1. LongPPL (arXiv:2410.23771) — FRAMING COLLISION, highest priority. It is the field's standard citation that perplexity does not track long-context ability, with measured numbers: standard PPL vs LongBench r = -0.11, LongEval +0.31, RULER +0.33; LongPPL reaches -0.96 / -0.86 / -0.84. Your entire damage law is calibrated against wikitext-2 perplexity. Every long-context reviewer will raise this reflexively. The technical rebuttal is real and you must make it explicitly: LongPPL measures ACROSS-MODEL correlation of ABSOLUTE perplexity against ABSOLUTE benchmark score; you measure WITHIN-MODEL RELATIVE degradation caused by a controlled perturbation, holding the model fixed. Those are different estimands and LongPPL never measures yours. But 'different estimand' is a paragraph you have to write, not a paragraph a reviewer will write for you. Strongest empirical fix: show the same curve predicts damage in LongPPL or in a RULER/NoLiMa/MiniLongBench score — MiniLongBench makes this affordable at 4.5% of LongBench cost with rho=0.97.

2. KVDiagnosis (arXiv:2608.09412, published 2026-08-10, eight days old) — CLOSEST LIVE COMPETITOR. It already ships the shape of your assets #2 and #3: cheap same-forward-pass diagnostics (including a Gold Perplexity Ratio, NLL delta, top-50 overlap, and an Evidence Attention Mass/Retention family), a demonstrated proxy-to-damage link (stratified AUROC 0.684-0.871 separating failed from successful compression), AND causal verification by intervention (a 4x evidence-attention boost repairs 29.2% of low-EAR failures vs 6.3% sham). It also publishes the 'aggregate scores hide different failure sets' result (SnapKV and TOVA both ~85 but Jaccard 0.38 on failures) — close to your 'cosine similarity is uncorrelated with real damage' point in spirit. What it does NOT do, by the authors' own admission: no noise-injection calibration, no quantitative damage-MAGNITUDE prediction (AUROC classification only), attention diagnostics and interventions 'remain Qwen-only', and cross-model generalization is 'limited for quantization-based approaches'. Your 26-model / 13-lineage atlas and calibrated x-damage law survive — but you must cite it, and 'we predict how much, not just whether' has to be the stated delta.

3. KV-CoRE (arXiv:2602.05929, Feb 2026) — DIRECT CLAIM OVERLAP on the shape 'cheap proxy metric predicts KV-compression damage, validated at scale'. They use Normalized Effective Rank from incremental SVD of KV caches, across multiple models, 5 English domains and 16 languages, and 'show that it correlates strongly with performance degradation under compression', explicitly billing themselves as 'the first large-scale benchmark of kv-cache compressibility'. If a reviewer reads only abstracts, yours and theirs sound like the same paper. Differentiators to state up front: rank-compressibility (an SVD/low-rank-compression coordinate) vs score-space noise (a quantization coordinate); correlation vs a calibrated per-model predictive curve; no catastrophe gate; no held-out bit-width extrapolation; no causal mechanism.

4. Physics of KV Cache Compression (arXiv:2603.01426, Mar 2026) — COLLIDES WITH THE CATASTROPHIC-REGIME NOVELTY. It reframes KV compression as 'a controlled perturbation of token-level routing' and reports 'a sharp hallucination safety cliff near 90% compression, correlated with spikes in Global Eviction Ratio (GER), suggesting a phase transition in semantic reachability', with distinct LLaMA vs Qwen resilience profiles. So 'controlled perturbation as the lens' + 'a catastrophic cliff exists' + 'architecture lineages differ' are all already in print. Your remaining novelty in the catastrophic regime is the CALIBRATED PREDICTION of it (R^2 0.971 

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- RULER, Limitations (Sec. 8), verbatim-adjacent: the benchmark 'reports a single number metric for each input length without providing the depth-level performance', so it cannot reveal lost-in-the-middle; and critically for anyone proposing a proxy metric — 'the lack of easy-to-evaluate realistic long-context tasks prevents us from verifying the validity of these proxies'. The authors also say they 'did not extend a comprehensive study on the prompt robustness', and frame RULER as 'convenient behavioral checks' that should not replace 'more realistic settings'. OPPORTUNITY: the proxy-validation gap they name is exactly the gap a calibrated damage law fills — but they are telling you the validation target itself is missing, so you must pick and defend one.
- HELMET: 'existing benchmarks often provide noisy signals due to limited coverage of applications, insufficient context lengths, unreliable metrics, and incompatibility with base models'; 'synthetic tasks like NIAH do not reliably predict downstream performance'; 'the diverse categories in HELMET exhibit distinct trends and low correlations with each other'. Stated future work on proxy selection: 'There may be more optimal methods for selecting synthetic datasets, and we leave this as future work.' OPPORTUNITY: HELMET's own answer to 'what cheap thing predicts downstream damage' is 'use our RAG tasks', which is still a full benchmark run — there is an open lane for a metric computed from the SAME forward pass.
- LongBench v2, stated limitations: 'The benchmark's size may not be sufficiently large' (503 samples, 100,000 CNY over two months to collect); the dataset is 'limited to English only'; and 'the length distribution across different tasks is uneven, with certain tasks concentrated around specific lengths'. On prior work: many datasets 'use metrics like ROUGE and F1 for evaluation, which are known to be unreliable', while LLM-as-a-judge 'can be costly and may introduce biases'. OPPORTUNITY: the field has no cheap, reliable, non-judge metric for long-context damage — which is precisely the vacancy a score-space coordinate could fill.
- LongPPL: no dedicated limitations section, but the authors acknowledge LongPPL costs 3-4x standard perplexity with an 8B evaluator model and substantially more with larger evaluators, and the metric is evaluator-model dependent (they report separate 72B and 8B evaluator correlations). OPPORTUNITY: an evaluator-free, forward-pass-internal substitute for LongPPL is an explicitly unsolved cost problem — and score-space attention-logit correlation is evaluator-free by construction.
- SCBench: no dedicated limitations section, but the authors flag two unresolved problems — attention distribution shift in long-generation scenarios, and the difficulty of query-dependent compression methods generalizing without query access. OPPORTUNITY: query-agnostic damage prediction (which is what a key-perturbation noise curve is) speaks directly to the second.
- KVDiagnosis (Aug 2026), stated limitations, verbatim-adjacent: coverage is limited to 'three logged revisions, greedy decoding, method-specific settings, fixed splits, and trace availability; attention AUROC and interventions remain Qwen-only'. Cross-model generalization is demonstrated for coverage diagnostics but 'noted as limited for quantization-based approaches'. OPPORTUNITY: this is the newest, closest work in the slice, and it names your two exact differentiators as its own gaps — cross-model breadth and quantization.
- Benchmarking KV-Cache Optimizations (arXiv:2607.05399), stated limitations: only 'models in the 7-8B parameter range'; 'exact memory usage, throughput, and latency will depend on system-level optimizations'; and TurboQuant runs 'take up to 3 days for one single run'. Stated future work: workload-aware selection of KV mechanisms, with a call for the data-management community's query-optimization experience. OPPORTUNITY: a pre-deployment checker that picks a configuration WITHOUT a 3-day run is literally the tool they are asking for.
- Positional Failures (arXiv:2605.23170), thirteen enumerated limitations including: 'N=50 per condition yields a 95% binomial confidence interval of approximately +/-14pp at p=0.5, limiting power for small drops'; 'Provider heterogeneity (five distinct API stacks) confounds cross-model magnitude comparisons with provider-level artifacts'; and 'The duplicate-at-end probe is a diagnostic consistency check, not a mitigation'. They commit to releasing CRE plus the 11-benchmark audit. OPPORTUNITY: an open-weights, single-GPU study has no provider-heterogeneity confound and can run far more than N=50 — a solo researcher's structural advantage over API-based audits.
- Distractor-Aware Truncation (arXiv:2608.03297), stated limitations: 'Three of our four models are from the Claude family'; 'All four benchmarks are English-only synthetic tasks'; 'Hand-crafted signal definitions' require per-benchmark engineering; mixed temperature settings across models; and a single deterministic seeded draw rather than multiple independent draws. Their conclusion is a standing methodological demand: 'future studies of context-length effects must specify how they distinguish signal from distractor, or they are at best ambiguous between two opposite hypotheses.'
- Chroma Context Rot (Jul 2025), stated limitations: the evaluation does not cover real-world complexity requiring synthesis or multi-step reasoning, where degradation would likely be MORE severe; and the authors explicitly lack a mechanistic explanation for the observed drops. OPPORTUNITY: 'no mechanism' is stated by the most-cited industry report in this slice, and a measured, causally-verified mechanism is asset #3.
- KVFundaBench / ShotKV (ICML 2026): the paper's framing is itself the open problem — KV-compression evaluation 'disproportionately focus[es] on sparse retrieval tasks, potentially masking the degradation of High-Density Reasoning where Chain-of-Thought coherence is critical', producing 'Task-Dependent Degradation'. Reasoning-coherence damage under compression remains largely unmeasured.
- The Pitfalls of KV Cache Compression (ACL 2026): 'The usual benchmarks for evaluating performance do not reflect more realistic applications of LLMs, instead focusing on single-instruction benchmarks like Q&A datasets, prompt retrieval tasks, and code generation.' They offer whitelisting and fair eviction as 'simple modifications' that 'suggest new directions for more sophisticated eviction policies' — i.e. the multi-instruction damage regime is wide open.
- MiniLongBench: the finding itself is an open problem for the field — 'existing LCU benchmarks exhibit significant redundancy'; 4.5% of LongBench's cost preserves rank correlation 0.97 across 60+ LLMs. Nobody has done the equivalent redundancy analysis for KV-compression evaluation suites.
- LOOM-Scope: 'variations in evaluation settings across these benchmarks lead to inconsistent results, making it difficult to draw reliable comparisons', plus 'the high computational cost of long-context evaluation poses a significant barrier for the community'. Standardization and cost remain unsolved enough that a framework paper was warranted in Jul 2025.

**Key works.**

- `arXiv:2410.02694 (ICLR 2025; v3 6 Mar 2025); github.com/princeton-nlp/HELMET (live, HTTP 200)` (2024-10-03 (v1), ICLR 2025) **HELMET: How to Evaluate Long-Context Language Models Effectively and Thoroughly** — Seven application-centric categories (recall, RAG, re-rank, citation, longQA, summarization, ICL), controllable lengths to 128K, model-based metrics, few-shot prompting so base models are evaluable. Evaluates 59 LCLMs. Findings: (1) 'synthetic tasks like NIAH do not reliably predict downstream perfo *Relevance:* THE reference for 'which long-context benchmark is trustworthy in 2026', and the methodological template you are implicitly copying: HELMET's whole contribution is 'correlate candidate cheap proxies against real downstream damage across many models'. Your atlas is the KV-perturbation analogue of HEL
- `arXiv:2404.06654 (COLM 2024)` (2024-04-09) **RULER: What's the Real Context Size of Your Long-Context Language Models?** — Configurable synthetic suite: 13 tasks over 4 categories (multi-needle retrieval, multi-hop variable tracking, aggregation, QA), flexible lengths. Headline: of 17 models all claiming >=32K, only about half maintain satisfactory performance at 32K, despite near-perfect vanilla NIAH. *Relevance:* Still the de facto stress test in 2026 and the benchmark the kvpress leaderboard reports on. Its authors' own Limitations section is unusually useful to you (see open_problems). Practically: RULER is the cheapest real long-context axis you can add on a 24 GB card, and it is what a KV-compression rev
- `arXiv:2412.15204` (2024-12 (v2 of the LongBench line)) **LongBench v2** — 503 human-curated multiple-choice questions, 8k-2M words, six categories. Human experts get 53.7% under a 15-minute limit; best model answering directly 50.1%; o1-preview 57.7%. Explicitly built because prior long-context data was 'not human-examined' and 'many of these samples can be solved through *Relevance:* The reference for 'realistic long-context reasoning' in 2026 and the reason LongBench v1 is now considered a legacy benchmark. Also the canonical citation that ROUGE/F1 metrics 'are known to be unreliable' — useful if you need to argue why you did NOT use LongBench v1 F1 as your damage target.
- `arXiv:2308.14508 (ACL 2024)` (2023-08-28) **LongBench: A Bilingual, Multitask Benchmark for Long Context Understanding** — 21 datasets, 6 categories, average 6,711 English words / 13,386 Chinese characters. Still the most-used benchmark in KV-compression papers. *Relevance:* This is what nearly every KV-compression paper reports, and it is the benchmark most criticized in 2026: average length ~6-18K (far below claimed context windows), F1/ROUGE metrics, no position control, contamination exposure. If you report LongBench you inherit all of those criticisms; if you don't
- `arXiv:2402.13718` (2024-02-21) **$\infty$Bench: Extending Long Context Evaluation Beyond 100K Tokens** — First benchmark with average length >100K tokens; synthetic + realistic tasks, English and Chinese. *Relevance:* Widely cited but now routinely described as having narrow domain coverage and noisy metrics (LongBench Pro and HELMET both make this argument). Treat as a legacy citation, not a headline result.

### Hybrid and linear-attention architectures (Mamba-2, Gated DeltaNet / Kimi Delta Attention, sliding-window and sparse-attention hybrids) and what they do to the KV-cache problem — i.e. whether KV quant

**State of the art.** Architecturally, the settled 2026 design is a layer-wise hybrid at a 3:1 to 8:1 ratio of efficient-to-full attention. Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B: 36 Gated DeltaNet + 12 full attention of 48 layers (verified from config.json: num_hidden_layers=48, full_attention_interval=4). Qwen3.5-397B-A17B: 45 recurrent + 15 attention of 60. Qwen3.6-27B: 48 Gated DeltaNet + 16 Gated Attention of 64. Kimi Linear-48B-A3B: 20 KDA + 7 MLA (and MLA compresses those further). Nemotron 3 Nano 30B-A3B: 6 attention of 52. Nemotron 3 Super 120B-A12B: 8 attention of 88. HySparse 80B: 5 full attention of 49.

The numbers that define the frontier: Kimi Linear reports 'up to 75% KV cache reduction' and 6x decode throughput at 1M context. HySparse reports ~10x KV storage reduction. Bick/Xing/Gu report 5-6x more memory-efficient hybrids by preserving only 2% of attention heads while recovering >95% of teacher retrieval performance. Measured per-token KV drops from 98,304 B/token (Qwen3-30B-A3B) to 20,480 (Qwen3.5-35B-A3B) to 6,144 (Nemotron-3-Nano). On recall/quality, 2507.06457 establishes that recall saturates at 3:1-6:1 linear:full and that language modeling is stable across ratios; 2606.15378 establishes mechanistically that long-range retrieval is carried by the full-attention layers. On consumer hardware, 2507.12442 measures 220K tokens on a single 24GB GPU with an SSM, ~4x a comparable Transformer.

For quantizing the recurrent side, SOTA is Quamba2 (W8A8/W4A8/W4A16 for Mamba1/2; 4x memory reduction at 1.6% average accuracy drop; 1.3x prefill / 3x generation speedup at 8B) and MambaQuant (W8A8 at <1% loss via KLT-enhanced rotation). Both target weights and activations. The persistent decode-time recurrent state is still FP32 by default in vLLM, and the ReplaySSM RFC explicitly lists FP16/FP8 state evaluation as work in progress.

So the honest answer to 'is KV quantization becoming irrelevant': the KV cache in a frontier model is roughly 5x-16x smaller than it was 18 months ago, purely from architecture. But (a) it still grows linearly with context in the surviving full-attention layers — 17.2 GB BF16 at 262K on Qwen3.6-27B, which is 64 layers with only 16 attention layers; (b) quantization composes multiplicatively with hybridization rather than competing with it; (c) a new, currently-uncompressed object appeared — the fixed-size recurrent state, ~75.5 MB/request FP32 for Qwen3-Next by my arithmetic, which dominates KV below ~3K tokens and multiplies by batch size; and (d) the field is 

**Collision risk.** NOTHING in this slice publishes a noise-response damage law for KV caches or recurrent states. I searched from six angles (perturbation/precision sensitivity of recurrent states; noise-injection predicting quantization degradation; attention-logit error predicting perplexity; bits-required-for-recurrent-state; hybrid quantization benchmarks; damage-law/noise-response-curve phrasing) and found no hybrid or linear-attention paper that injects calibrated noise and reads off predicted damage. The core asset is not collided with in my territory. The risks are these, ranked:

1. CONCEPTUAL, HIGH — arXiv:2606.26488 (2026-06-25), 'What Survives When You Compress a Recursive Reasoner for the Edge?'. Same three-move structure as the researcher's asset: a catastrophic regime that aggregate metrics miss (cell accuracy holds while puzzle-exact accuracy collapses to zero), a cheap label-free proxy that predicts damage BEFORE task evaluation ('carry-trajectory fidelity, the cosine similarity to the full-precision reasoning path'), and error compounding through recurrence. Different domain (million-parameter recursive reasoners), different proxy, no noise-injection calibration curve, no cross-model law, no held-out bit-width prediction. But 'label-free fidelity signal predicts compression damage and recovery' is now published. Any abstract sentence of the form 'we introduce a cheap proxy that predicts compression damage before evaluation' will read as a rediscovery unless explicitly distinguished. Cite it.

2. FRAMING, HIGH — arXiv:2606.27791 (NLL-guided full-attention layer selection, 2026-06-26) plus arXiv:2512.20569 (KL-guided layer selection, 2025-12-23). Both measure a per-layer degradation score cheaply and use it to allocate a scarce resource across layers, and 2606.27791 even includes a de-confounding analysis separating long-range need from generic layer sensitivity. This kills any novelty claim phrased as 'first to allocate per-layer treatment by measured damage'. It does NOT touch the noise-calibrated mapping, the score-space coordinate, the catastrophe gate, or held-out bit-width extrapolation. The researcher's layer_adaptive.py positioning specifically needs to be re-argued against these.

3. CLAIM-OVERLAP, MEDIUM-HIGH — arXiv:2607.08734 (2026-07-09), 'The Illusion of Equivalency'. Independently reports 'non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths' and that 'query and key projections are consistently more sensitive than value and output projections', and argues accuracy/perplexity 'fail to capture behavioral changes induced by quantization'. That is three of the researcher's rhetorical beats (catastrophic regime, K-side fragility, standard metrics mislead) in one July-2026 paper. It is weights-not-KV, has no predictive law, and its metric (correctness agreement) is decision-level not score-space — but the language collision is real and recent.

4. METHOD-OVERLAP, MEDIUM — arXiv:2606.03458 KVarN (2026-06-02, Huawei, vLLM implementation released). Hadamard rotation + variance normalization for 2-bit KV, explicitly framed around decode-time error accumulation. This overlaps the WHT-rotation production cache asset (rotation is now table stakes; Quamba and MambaQuant also use Hadamard/KLT rotations, so rotation is table stakes on the recurrent side too) and it lands the critique that prefill-style evaluation is the wrong regime. Since the damage law is calibrated on wikitext-2 perplexity, KVarN is the paper a reviewer will use to ask whether the law survives long autoregressive decoding. Worth pre-empting with a decode-regime spot check.

5. PROVENANCE, MEDIUM AND URGENT — two artifacts bearing the researcher's own naming appear in third-party hands: kaitchup is benchmarking a vLLM KV format called `turboquant_4bit_nc` head-to-head against `int4_per_token_head` and `nvfp4` on Qwen3.6-27B (paywalled, so I could not read the results), and arXiv:2604.16957 is titled 'Open-TQ-Metal: Fused Compressed-Domain Attention for Long-Context LLM Infe

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- 2507.06457 (A Systematic Analysis of Hybrid Linear Attention), Limitations, verbatim: 'Our analysis is confined to models up to 1.3B parameters, a 4,096-token context window, and block-wise mixing ratios.' And: 'The question of whether or not the observed trade-offs persist at the 10B+ scale, with 128k token contexts, or under instruction-tuning and multilingual data remains open.' Section 4.3 adds that findings are 'necessarily observational' and 'Ablations are left to future work.' Their conclusion flags 'head-wise or dynamic routing' and 'automated architecture search' as unexplored. NOTE the asymmetry this creates: an inference-only researcher with 26 published checkpoints operates at exactly the 10B+/128k scale these authors say they cannot reach.
- 2606.15378 (Rethinking the Role of Efficient Attention in Hybrid Architectures), Limitations, verbatim: 'the largest model we train is still at the sub-billion-parameter level with at most ~100B pretraining tokens'; they 'pretrain directly at a 16K context length and extend to at most 32K, in contrast to the prevailing recipe'; they 'cover representative operators widely adopted in recent hybrid architectures, while leaving out some other popular variants such as RWKV-7 and Kimi-Linear'; and 'design choices discussed in Section 6 are intended to validate our mechanistic conclusions rather than to serve as a full design study, and a more comprehensive verification at larger scales is left to future work.' They perform NO precision or quantization analysis whatsoever, despite establishing that full-attention layers carry all long-range retrieval.
- vLLM ReplaySSM RFC (#47572): current evaluation is FP32 state, described as 'default in most hybrid models', with lower-precision (FP16/FP8) evaluation explicitly listed as work-in-progress. No quantization measurements are provided; the authors only note a structural argument that a low-precision checkpoint would be re-quantized ~L times less often than a per-step baseline. This is a named, dated, unclaimed gap in the exact serving stack the asking researcher already patches.
- 2606.18114 (Ternary Mamba): authors report that post-hoc correction strategies effective for Transformers 'prove ineffective for State Space Models due to error propagation through recurrence mechanisms', and document a previously undocumented 'zero-ratio collapse' instability specific to pretrained-checkpoint QAT. Open: no principled account of how much error a recurrence tolerates before collapse.
- 2407.12397 (Mamba-PTQ), verbatim: 'While these techniques [quantization and pruning] are well-established for attention-based models, their effects on recurrent layers remain underexplored.' Self-described as 'preliminary work' that reports baselines 'that do not take into account the activation outliers' and only 'suggest first steps for outlier-aware quantization.' Two years old and still the honest state of the recurrent-state precision question.
- 2606.26488 (What Survives When You Compress a Recursive Reasoner): 'unlike conventional sequence models, quantization errors compound across recursive reasoning cycles rather than across output tokens. As a result, standard intuitions about compression fail to apply.' They also find 'Token-level objectives, including quantization-aware training, cannot repair it' and that 'The collapse is architectural — it strikes MLP-mixing recursion but not attention on the same task.' Open: whether this compounding law has a closed form, and whether it holds for LLM-scale hybrids rather than million-parameter reasoners.
- 2607.07953 (Linear Attention Architectures survey), verbatim: 'The reported speed results measure training throughput and iteration time; we do not provide an empirical inference-speed benchmark.' They also report that the most natural DeltaNet-inspired cross-layer routing 'does not improve over matched baselines'. Deployment-side measurement of these architectures is openly unclaimed.
- 2505.07793 (Overflow Prevention), verbatim: 'our findings raise questions about whether recurrent models genuinely exploit long-range dependencies, as our single-chunk strategy delivers stronger performance - even in tasks that presumably require cross-context relations.' Open: whether the recurrent-state long-context claim is real at all, which directly bounds how far KV compression can be declared obsolete.
- 2410.07145 (Stuffed Mamba): recurrent state capacity in passkey retrieval 'scales exponentially to the state size'; state collapse is attributed to 'overfitting due to the recurrent state being overparameterized for the training length'. Open: no mapping from state precision (as opposed to state size) to capacity, which is precisely the axis a noise-response curve measures.
- 2602.11374 (Retrieval-Aware Distillation): retrieval-critical heads are identified 'via ablation on a synthetic retrieval task' — an expensive, task-specific, ground-truth-requiring procedure. They also find 'large recurrent states often compensate for missing retrieval'. Open: no cheap, task-free, calibrated sensitivity signal for which heads matter, and no analysis of what precision those 2% of surviving heads require.
- 2606.27791 (NLL-Guided Full-Attention Layer Selection): calibration takes ~15 minutes and is demonstrated on one model (Qwen3-4B) and one benchmark (LongMemEval). The abstract states no limitations; the generalization of NLL-based layer scores across models, context lengths, and to precision allocation rather than attention-type allocation is untouched.
- 2606.03458 (KVarN): 'current methods are evaluated under prefill-like settings and errors behave differently under autoregressive decoding' — quantization errors 'accumulate across timesteps, driven primarily by incorrect token scales'. Open (and a direct threat to any perplexity-only atlas): whether a damage law calibrated on teacher-forced perplexity predicts damage under long autoregressive decode.
