---

### vLLM KV-cache internals as of Aug 2026: which `--kv-cache-dtype` values exist, which attention backends accept them, how PagedAttention's block/page allocator constrains a custom KV byte layout, and t

**State of the art.** MERGED AND SHIPPING (the bar to clear): TurboQuant in vLLM, merged 2026-04-15. The defining number is the k8v4 preset — 2.6x KV compression (196 bytes/slot at head_dim=128) while holding 79-100% of BF16 throughput across six serving scenarios, GSM8K 0.860 vs 0.900 FP16, NIAH 100%, and >98% average accuracy recovery on reasoning benchmarks per vLLM's own blog. It even beats BF16 TPOT on long prefill (135.2 vs 138.1 ms) because the compressed cache relieves memory-bandwidth pressure. The aggressive end, 3bit_nc, reaches 4.9x (104 B/slot) but pays 65-93% throughput and drops GSM8K to 0.720 with ~20-point collapses on AIME25/LiveCodeBench-v6.

Also merged: int4_per_token_head (2026-06-24), Hadamard pre-rotation + asymmetric quantization with a per-(token,head) zero-point steganographically packed into the fp32 scale mantissa, GSM8K 0.912 vs 0.940 FP16 on Qwen3.5-35B-A3B. And nvfp4 / nvfp4_4over6 KV on Blackwell.

CONTESTED, NOT YET MERGED (where the bar is heading): KVarN (arXiv:2606.03458, PR #46812) claims ~3.2-3.4x at int4-K/int2-V with throughput AT OR ABOVE FP16 in the memory-bound regime (+9% at 14B, +18% at ~106B) — the only sub-4-bit backend making that claim — calibration-free and measured lossless on Qwen3-14B/30B and GLM-4.5-Air, plus MLA/hybrid/SWA coverage TurboQuant lacks. SQuat (PR #44564) adds learned per-layer correction matrices on top of TurboQuant 4bit-nc for +1.0-3.4pp on MATH500 at <1% overhead.

THE HONEST FRAMING OF WHERE THE FIELD IS. Compression ratio has stopped being the scarce quantity; the vLLM community has settled that at 2.6-4.9x and moved on. Two things are scarce. (1) Throughput neutrality — KVarN's RFC states the field's own verdict: 'sub-4-bit KV quantization is still rarely turned on in production, for one reason: it costs throughput and accuracy... Losing both speed and quality is why the feature stays off.' (2) Knowing which configuration to run. There are now nine-plus KV dtypes, four TurboQuant presets, four KVarN presets, a --kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers flag whose only documented default is the hand-waved heuristic 'sliding-window layers are more sensitive', and no principled selection rule anywhere in the stack. vLLM's own maintainers have an open, unclaimed checkbox for exactly this. That second scarcity, not the first, is where a solo researcher with 176 measured cells and a validated predictive law has an actual edge.

**Collision risk.** Be direct: this slice contains the worst collision of the whole campaign, and it is a name-and-algorithm collision, not a near-miss.

1. FATAL TO ANY CODEC-NOVELTY CLAIM — "TurboQuant" is already vLLM's, shipping since 2026-04-15. vLLM main exposes `--kv-cache-dtype turboquant_k8v4|turboquant_4bit_nc|turboquant_k3v4_nc|turboquant_3bit_nc`, backed by turboquant_attn.py (1272 lines), triton_turboquant_{decode,store}.py, a FlyDSL AMD gfx950 decode path, a structure-of-arrays variant directory, and vllm/model_executor/layers/quantization/turboquant/. The founding PR (#38479) describes its own method as "PolarQuant (WHT rotation + Lloyd-Max scalar quantization) for keys, uniform quantization for values", and specifically documents replacing QR-decomposed random orthogonal matrices with WHT + random sign flips. That is the asking researcher's production cache, component for component, merged four months ago, under the same name, with a vLLM blog post and NIAH/GSM8K/RULER/LongBench evals. The working-code asset (item 4: WHT + Lloyd-Max + residual signs, 5.54x after the bit-packing fix) cannot be presented as a novel artifact. It is a compatible independent implementation at best. The project name itself will read to any vLLM-adjacent reviewer as a fork of an upstream feature.

2. SEVERE, AND FRESH — layer-wise KV bit allocation from per-layer sensitivity now has a production implementation. NVIDIA/Model-Optimizer PR #2211 ("Add layer-wise KV-cache AutoQuant with forward KL") and its vLLM consumer PR #52813 were both filed 2026-08-18 — the day before this survey. ModelOpt quantizes one attention layer at a time against a BF16 no-KV-quant logit reference, scores isolated forward KL, solves a width-weighted additive recipe under a kv_effective_bits budget, and exports heterogeneous per-layer KV formats plus "a JSON-safe sensitivity report" as versioned checkpoint metadata that vLLM dispatches per layer. This is the same job as the damage law. The defensible distinctions, and they must be argued explicitly in the paper rather than assumed: (a) theirs is a search costing O(layers x formats) calibrated forward passes; the damage law is calibrated once per model by quantizer-free noise injection and then evaluated in closed form at any configuration's measured score-space noise; (b) forward KL gives a relative ranking, not a predicted perplexity multiplier with an error bar — no x1.01 median error, no x5 catastrophe gate, no held-out bit-widths; (c) they assume per-layer sensitivity is ADDITIVE, an assumption the researcher's 96-configuration dataset can directly test and, if it fails, refute; (d) their format menu is FP8/NVFP4 only, i.e. >=4 bits, so it never enters the x1348-damage catastrophic regime the law was built for. Novelty survives contact with this — but only if #2211 is cited and distinguished. Ignoring it is the single most likely way this contribution gets desk-rejected.

3. SEVERE — the mechanism's remedy is partly upstream already. Merged PR #40835 (2026-06-24) ships INT4_PER_TOKEN_HEAD with "a random Hadamard pre-rotation to make the inputs more Gaussian, then asymmetric quantization with a 4-bit zero-point steganographically stored in the low 4 bits of the float32 scale mantissa. The asymmetry is corrected on the score side with a sum(Q) * zp term", and INT2_PER_TOKEN_HEAD with "a full Hadamard pre-rotation and a Lloyd-Max centroid lookup... 4 centroids fitted over N(0,1)". So: Hadamard rotation, Lloyd-Max over N(0,1), AND a real asymmetric offset with score-side correction are all merged. The statement "vLLM cannot handle the key offset" is now false. What survives: theirs is a per-(token,head) dynamic zero-point that absorbs the offset numerically without ever identifying it; the researcher's contribution is the DIAGNOSIS — a per-head SHARED mean, decomposed into k_proj bias at boundary layers plus massive activations in middle layers, traced across five Qwen generations, and reproduced on demand by injection into an im

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- vLLM issue #40069, Accuracy section, verbatim and STILL UNCHECKED as of 2026-08-18: '- [ ] Per-layer sensitivity sweep to inform `--kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers` defaults'. This is the asking researcher's damage law, requested by the maintainer who owns the feature, unclaimed for four months. The actuator is already merged and takes bare layer indices, so the deliverable is a list of integers per model plus the rule that produced it — zero kernel work.
- vLLM issue #40069, verbatim, unchecked: '- [ ] Publish recommended config table (quality vs. compression vs. throughput) based on eval results'. This is the 176-cell atlas, requested by name. The researcher's atlas is missing the throughput axis (it pairs perplexity with same-forward-pass proxies), but vLLM's blog already published the throughput column for all four presets — the two compose.
- vLLM issue #40069, verbatim, unchecked: '- [ ] Add new presets as the sweeps suggest (e.g. mixed-bit, per-layer schedules)'. Mixed-bit per-layer schedules named as future work. Caveat before celebrating: NVIDIA ModelOpt #2211 + vLLM #52813, filed 2026-08-18, are a credible attempt to fill this from the search-based direction.
- vLLM TurboQuant blog (2026-05-11), verbatim: 'TurboQuant supports only models with standard attention mechanisms (e.g. GQA) — models with sliding-window or hybrid attention are not yet supported.' Partially addressed by PR #39931 (merged 2026-05-05, 'support hybrid models and uniform quantization') but the tracking issue's 'Hybrid attention models (e.g. Qwen3.5, mamba+attention, interleaved SWA?)' box remains unchecked and bugs keep landing (#50709, #52475, #41726).
- vLLM TurboQuant blog (2026-05-11): aggressive variants lose 'up to 8 points' AUC at 64k and show 'drastic accuracy drops of ~20 points' on AIME25/LiveCodeBench-v6, which the authors attribute to the fact that 'low-bit KV-cache quantization errors accumulate with sequence length'. Note what this concedes: vLLM has NO model of how damage scales with context length. The researcher's noise-response curves are measured at a single context; extending the law with a sequence-length term addresses a limitation the vLLM authors state in their own words and cannot currently predict.
- vLLM issue #40069, Backend coverage: '- [ ] MLA support (through a new attention backend?)'. Contributor shanyulu claimed it on 2026-04-29 and by 2026-05-06 had a CUDA-only TRITON_MLA_TURBOQUANT prototype validated on DeepSeek-V2-Lite across 4x RTX 4090 at TP=4, with WikiText-2 PPL sanity checks. Worth noting for the asking researcher: another solo contributor is doing serious vLLM TurboQuant work on the same GPU model.
- vLLM issue #40069, Feature compatibility, both unchecked: '- [ ] Speculative decoding / Eagle' and '- [ ] KV connector / disaggregated serving (NIXL, LMCache, Mooncake)'. Concrete failures filed against both: #40807 (CUDA-graph capture crash at turboquant_attn.py:570 on query_start_loc.tolist(), spec-dec + chunked-prefill) and #40831/#52475 (MTP x TurboQuant produces degenerate token loops on Qwen3-Next hybrid — 'while either feature alone works correctly'). The MTP interaction is a silent quality failure, not a crash, which is exactly the class of bug a damage-law catastrophe gate is built to catch.
- vLLM issue #40069, Performance: '- [ ] CUDA/cutedsl kernels to replace the triton kernels'. The merged backend is Triton-only, which is why it pays 10-68% latency overhead. This is where the throughput gap to KVarN actually lives.
- vLLM issue #40069, comment from user gaby (2026-04-24), verbatim: 'Now that vllm 0.20.0 was released, it is not documented which models are supported. Every model I have tried so far fails to start.' And earlier (2026-04-17): 'It would be good to know which models even support this. I brought testing with modern models multiple times in #38479, but never got a straight answer. Right now, most modern models are Hybrid/MoE, and they all fail to start when using the new KV quantization methods.' Confirmed by inspection: docs/features/quantization/quantized_kvcache.md on main is still FP8-only and never mentions TurboQuant; PR #45422 'docs: add TurboQuant KV cache presets' is still open. A per-model support-and-safety matrix is an open, explicitly-requested, documentation-shaped contribution — and it is exactly what kvcheck.py plus the 26-model atlas already are.
- vLLM RFC #51751 (2026-08-11), verbatim on the integration cost: 'These are hard-coded. Adding a new KV cache dtype requires modifying vLLM core in at least four places... This makes it impossible for: Hardware backends — to ship their own KV cache quantization format without editing vLLM... Third-party quantization libraries — to integrate directly.' Also flags an existing wart: 'The duplicate is_quantized_kv_cache helpers (vllm/v1/kv_cache_interface.py:77-83 and vllm/utils/torch_utils.py:75-85 — currently two parallel implementations) should be merged.' The implementing PR #52670 was opened 2026-08-18 with an empty Test Plan and empty Test Result.
- vLLM hybrid KV cache manager design doc, stated limitations: 'Case 4 padding strategy is still a work in progress'; no support for models without full attention layers or with 3+ attention types; Mamba prefix caching is 'work in progress'; potential 'non-negligible overheads' when sliding-window cache checks find no matches. Combined with kv_cache_utils.py's hard `raise NotImplementedError('The page size of the layer is not divisible by the maximum page size. Cannot unify by adjusting block_size.')`, this is the precise systems constraint on any layer-adaptive bit schedule: per-layer page sizes must divide the maximum, and unifying silently changes prefix-caching granularity for the cheaper layers.
- In-tree TODO, vllm/v1/kv_cache_interface.py: `# TODO(Lucas/Chen): less hacky way to do this` on MLAAttentionSpec.cache_dtype_str, the hook carrying the hardcoded `block_size * 656` for fp8_ds_mla. On main this has been generalized to AttentionSpec.state_content_bytes + customize_spec(), and a second TODO remains on real_page_size_bytes: 'Alias of unpadded_page_size_bytes. TODO(lucas): follow up with TPU backend to see if we can remove this property.'
- compressed-tensors KV path, stated in code: `raise NotImplementedError('Only support symmetric scaling factor for compressed-tensors KV cache')`; `raise NotImplementedError('Currently supported kv cache quantization is num_bits=8, type=float')`; and in create_weights, 'Zero points are not used in vLLM as currently only symmetric quantization is supported. We need to create them here to enable loading of llm-compressor checkpoints which contain them irrespective of the symmetric/asymmetric scheme used during quantization', with the loader early-returning on zero_point. There is per-head SCALE (strategy 'attn_head') but no per-head OFFSET anywhere in the checkpoint path. The researcher's per-head shared key mean is precisely an offset that a symmetric-only pipeline cannot represent — a clean, code-level statement of an unserved need.
- vLLM docs, the only stated basis for the skip-layers default, verbatim: 'Some attention layer types (e.g. sliding-window) are more sensitive to KV-cache quantization.' No measurement, no citation, no per-model data. PR #47604 is a straight revert of #42890 ('Support nvfp4 kv with kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers sliding_window'), and #42345 is a separate open attempt at 'excluding SWA layers from NVFP4 KV cache' — evidence the heuristic is unstable in practice.
- NVIDIA ModelOpt #2211 self-stated scope limit: FP8-K/NVFP4-V within a single layer 'requires the independent mixed-K/V attention-kernel implementation. Neither PR bundles that kernel work', and vLLM #52813 'intentionally rejects' it. So intra-layer mixed K/V precision — the researcher's asymmetric keys-vs-values treatment — has no kernel in either project and is explicitly disclaimed by both.

---

### Systems/kernels slice: GPU attention kernels that consume compressed KV — FlashAttention/FlashDecoding variants with quantized KV, dequantization-free (lookup-table / codebook-domain) attention, fused

**State of the art.** Three answers depending on what "best" means. (1) SHIPPED: FP8 KV cache with FP8 QK and ScoreV, default-recommended in vLLM as of 2026-04-22, mirrored in SGLang/FlashInfer, with NVFP4 KV on Blackwell — the defining number is per-token decode KV cost at 54% of BF16 (i.e. ~2x), near-baseline accuracy, and a decode ITL slope roughly halved; FlashInfer XQA decode is 1.3-1.7x faster than FA3 on FP8 KV at batch>=32. (2) FASTEST RESEARCH KERNEL: BitDecoding (HPCA 2026) at 2/4-bit — average 7.5x decode speedup over FP16 FlashDecoding-v2, up to 8.6x on Blackwell NVFP4, 4.3x over QServe, 3x single-batch latency reduction on LLaMA-3.1-8B at 128K, measured on RTX 4090 / A100 / H100 / Blackwell. (3) BEST DEPLOYABLE ACCURACY-PER-BIT WITH A KERNEL: OSCAR at 2.28 effective bits/element in SGLang — mean gap to BF16 of 3.78 / 1.42 / -0.02 / +0.27 points on Qwen3-4B-Thinking / Qwen3-8B / Qwen3-32B / GLM-4.7-FP8, ~8x KV memory reduction, up to 7x throughput at large batch, 3x faster batch-1 decode; and SAW-INT4 at 4 bits with a fused rotation kernel showing -0.6% kernel overhead versus plain INT4 and +41.4% system TPS over BF16 at concurrency 64. For the researcher's specific stack, the relevant SOTA is llama.cpp turbo3: 3.47x compression at 98.5% of F16 decode on RTX 5090 and +0.44% wikitext-2 PPL on sm_89 (RTX 4090). Nothing in the slice reaches 5x+ real compression at near-lossless quality with a kernel that is also faster than FP16 — that combination is still open, and Block-GTQ's 3.24x at 1.34x-faster-than-FA2 is the closest published point.

**Collision risk.** "Five live collisions, ranked.\n\n1. AXELRAM (arXiv:2604.02638, Apr 2026) — the most damaging. It independently publishes (a) dequantization-free attention on the *identical* TurboQuant construction (orthogonal transform -> coordinates ~N(0,1/d) -> Lloyd-Max codebook that depends only on (d,b), 30 bytes at b=3, d=128), (b) a CATASTROPHIC regime discovery with the same model shape as the researcher's mechanism story — 'sign pattern sensitivity causes catastrophic PPL spikes (Δ>50) on certain models (Qwen2.5-3B), while others (LLaMA 3.1-8B) are fully stable' — and (c) a competing pre-deployment checker: 'check the layer-wise key norm ratio (max/min across layers)... Ratio <2x: calibration-free is safe. Ratio >5x: sign pattern optimization is recommended.' That is kvcheck.py's job description, published in April. What it does NOT have: the score-space coordinate, a continuous calibrated damage law with an R^2, a 26-model/176-cell atlas, held-out bit-width prediction, or the per-head shared-key-mean mechanism (their diagnosed cause is layer-wise norm heterogeneity interacting with the rotation's random sign seed, and their fix is search over 200 sign candidates). Sinks a bare 'catastrophe on Qwen, not on Llama, with a cheap checker' novelty claim; does not sink the quantitative law.\n\n2. WitCert (arXiv:2607.28699, Jul 2026, v3 Aug 16 2026 — two days ago). Same opening framing verbatim ('validated today by offline benchmark averages; a deployed system cannot tell whether compression is damaging the request it is serving right now'), same target artifact (a per-(layer,head,step) quantity that tells you whether a quantizer is safe), shipped inside SGLang, Lean-4-checked, with gating that restores raw-cast fp8 from 22.8 to 79.7 on RULER. It is a SOUND UPPER BOUND, not a calibrated predictor — that is the surviving differentiator, plus the 96-config/13-model regression fit and the x1.01 median accuracy of a point estimate versus a worst-case bound. But its 28-layer sweep result ('no single layer's pollution alone loses anything (0/28)'; 'aggressive schemes survive on cross-layer error cancellation, not per-step fidelity') is an aimed shot at a WORST-LAYER coordinate and must be addressed head-on, not ignored. Companion: Runtime-Certified Bounded-Error Quantized Attention (2605.20868) occupies the same niche with per-head/per-step bounds driving adaptive precision.\n\n3. llama.cpp feature/turboquant-kv-cache (turbo2/turbo3/turbo4) — verified commits in ggml-org/llama.cpp. WHT rotation + Lloyd-Max centroids + a separate sign-bit plane + real sub-byte bit-packing (4 indices/byte, 8 signs/byte) + fused flash-attention kernels (vec_dot_fattn_vec_KQ_turbo3_0, dequantize_V_turbo3_0) across CUDA, Metal, Vulkan and HIP, with 3.47x compression at 98.5% of F16 decode on RTX 5090 and +0.44% PPL / 229 vs 384 MiB KV on sm_89 (the researcher's own 4090). Plus turboquant-vllm's fused_paged_tq4_attention Triton kernel reading vLLM page tables in rotated space, OmarHory/turboquant's FusedQuantizedAttentionCUDA + packing.py, tqai v0.5's bit-packing (2/3/4/6-bit) + fused dequant-attention Metal kernels, and TurboESM's Triton kernel that already fuses 3-bit unpack + QJL correction + online softmax. Asset #4 (production KV cache fixed to 5.54x via bit-packing) has essentially no novelty left as an artifact; its value is now as instrumentation for the measurement work, not as a systems contribution.\n\n4. FlashInfer issue #2900 (Mar 2026) — the exact fused TurboQuant_prod decode kernel, including the <q, Pi^T C> = <Pi q, C> rotate-query-once identity and the two-part storage format, is publicly requested with the math written out, and official Google code was expected Q2 2026. 'We built the fused kernel' is closed. The open sub-problem the issue itself poses — how a two-part (indices + sign plane + two fp16 norms) code should live in a paged KV layout — is NOT closed.\n\n5. Score-space-as-correct-coordinate is more crowded than the HeadQ/RateQuant prior art

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- Block-GTQ (2606.24033), verbatim: 'Block-GTQ allocates bits only on K, leaving V uniform. A V-side allocator, joint K+V optimization, and denser packing could further reduce memory.' And: 'The fused decode path is an initial single-GPU implementation; multi-GPU and batched serving are open directions.' — A single-4090 researcher is well-positioned on exactly the single-GPU denser-packing half of this.
- LOOKAT (2601.10155), verbatim limitations: 'LOOKAT's primary limitations is that it only compresses keys, leaving values in FP16. Another limitation is that codebook quality depends on calibration data and how it is represented... Hardware specific kernel implementations on NPUs/DSPs are required.' Future work: 'extending product quantization to value compression. This is non trivial because of the weighted sum requirements.' — And note the paper reports zero perplexity numbers on GPT-2 only, up to 1024 tokens, with no kernel built.
- AXELRAM (2604.02638), verbatim: 'No hardware has been fabricated for AXELRAM. All results in this paper are derived from bit-accurate software simulation.' / 'Physical implementation and silicon validation remain as future work.' / 'On the 2-bit limitation of Qwen3-8B. After sign pattern optimization, Qwen3-8B at 2-bit still exhibits Δ=+9.26... The root cause is that 2-bit quantization provides only 4 representable levels, which is insufficient to capture the distribution tails of Qwen3-8B's key vectors.' — There is no GPU kernel here at all, and its predictor (layer-wise key-norm max/min ratio) is a 3-model heuristic with two hand-set thresholds and no calibration curve.
- TurboESM (2603.26110), verbatim: 'The Triton kernel currently targets single-batch decode; multi-batch decode requires further engineering.' / 'For short protein sequences typical in ESM-2 workloads (32-165 tokens), the KV fetch is not the dominant cost, so the practical end-to-end benefit is limited.' / 'All experiments are conducted on ESM-2 650M; validation on larger PLM variants remains as future work.' Plus a stated 21-27 ms prefill overhead. — The honest admission that a fused 3-bit+QJL Triton kernel does not yet produce end-to-end speedup is itself a stated open problem.
- BitDecoding (2503.18773): no Limitations section exists; the conclusion names 'algorithm-system co-design for KV-cache quantization' as future work. What it silently does not address: vector quantization, Hadamard/rotation-based schemes, 1-bit codes, and non-power-of-two bit widths — a 3- or 3.5-bit packed layout has no tensor-core-friendly treatment anywhere in this line of work.
- SAW-INT4 (2604.19157): no Limitations or Future Work section. The paper's own conclusion is a challenge rather than a gap — 'complexity yields diminishing returns. Our experiments reveal that orthogonal rotation is the single most important ingredient' — and it acknowledges only that block-diagonal rotation 'trades off quantization quality against system efficiency' (smaller blocks favour parallelism but sacrifice mixing quality). The unclaimed territory is the mixing-quality/parallelism frontier they leave as a knob.
- WitCert (2607.28699): the probabilistic certificate is stated only 'for non-adaptive queries'; the deterministic tier is a worst-case Cauchy-Schwarz bound, i.e. sound but loose, and gives no calibrated estimate of actual damage. Its own analysis result names the deeper open question: 'aggressive schemes survive on cross-layer error cancellation, not per-step fidelity — in a 28-layer sweep, no single layer's pollution alone loses anything (0/28).' Nobody has explained or modelled that cancellation.
- Runtime-Certified Bounded-Error Quantized Attention (2605.20868), verbatim: 'The certification is local (per-head, per-step) and does not guarantee end-to-end model correctness.' It also flags that value-sensitive tasks at short contexts expose compression/fidelity tradeoffs. The bound-to-end-to-end-damage link is explicitly disclaimed by both certification papers in this slice — that link is precisely what a calibrated damage law provides.
- vLLM FP8 blog (2026-04-22), authors' own caveats: prefill still regresses for head_dim=256; hybrid models with small sliding-window layers are faster left in BF16 (hence the new --kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers flag); and 'models or backends that show persistent uncalibrated accuracy loss, where calibration remains important.' The mechanism by which some layers/models are unsafe is not modelled — the production answer today is a manually curated skip-list.
- FlashInfer issue #2900 open design questions posed by the requester and unanswered: 'TurboQuant_prod's two-part representation doesn't fit the standard single tensor with quantized dtype format. Would you prefer (a) a new dedicated cache layout, (b) extending existing paged KV cache metadata, or (c) a separate wrapper?' — the storage-format problem for two-part (indices + sign plane + two norms) codes in paged serving engines is genuinely unsolved and is a systems contribution nobody has claimed.
- OSCAR SGLang PR #32129 thread: a user's BFCL multi-turn evaluation shows overall accuracy 41.12 -> 30.54 and long-context 34.67 -> 22.00 versus BF16, only partially recovered by re-tuning recent-window size and enabling Lloyd-Max buckets. The maintainers have no principled way to predict which workload/model/window combination will fall off — the config search is manual. Also listed as unsupported: MLA models, hybrid linear-attention models, speculative decoding, PD-disaggregation, DCP, hybrid-SWA.

- `arXiv:2606.24033 (2026-06-23) — VERIFIED (abs + html fetched)` (2026-06-23) **RoPE-Aware Bit Allocation for KV-Cache Quantization (Block-GTQ)** — Builds directly on TurboQuant-MSE. ### Sensitivity estimation for compression — how the field decides "which parameter / layer / head / channel matters" before allocating bits. Two lineages: (a) derivative-based, from OBD/OBS through HAWQ 

**State of the art.** Two answers, because the slice splits.

DERIVATIVE LINEAGE (the default): for weights, layerwise Hessian (GPTQ) and Fisher/gradient-square diagonal (SqueezeLLM) are the standard sensitivity signals; HAWQ-V2's Hessian trace is the canonical mixed-precision allocator. For the KV cache the state of the art is RateQuant (arXiv:2605.06675, Apr 2026): per-head sensitivity w_i = E_x E_t ||grad_{K_{i,t}} L||^2 from a 16-sequence calibration set (~1.6 s on an 8B model), combined with an exponential distortion model D(b)=alpha*beta^{-b} and reverse waterfilling b_i* = b_bar + (ln w_i - ln w_bar)/ln beta. Its defining number is beta = 3.6–5.3 across quantizer designs, with the finding that using the wrong quantizer's beta inverts the allocation and does worse than uniform.

MEASUREMENT LINEAGE (the answer to "is sensitivity ever measured by noise injection?" — yes): the defining number is HIGGS's linearity theorem, E[PPL] ~= PPL* + sum_l alpha_l t_l^2, calibrated by i.i.d. Gaussian noise insertion into weights and explicitly valid only for b > 3.0 (arXiv:2411.17525). In the KV cache the state of the art is eleven days old: RippleKV (arXiv:2608.08684, 9 Aug 2026) injects v + alpha*||v||_2*eps, eps~N(0,I) into each layer's value cache, reads the output KL over a small calibration set, and reports the perturbation response correlates 0.799 with layer importance versus 0.359 for layer depth — one magnitude, one metric, allocation only.

The bridging result that makes the measurement lineage necessary rather than merely convenient is ReQuant/PQI (arXiv:2503.01901): gradient and Hessian sensitivity 'underestimate quantization's impact on the loss function by orders of magnitude' — measured at 6.88e-03 true vs -5.98e-06 predicted on Llama 3.2 1B — 'due to the small convergence radius of local 2nd order approximation'.

Nothing published fits a per-model noise-response curve in attention-logit (score) space, evaluates it at measured quantizer configurations, and reports predictive accuracy across a catastrophic damage range. The best comparable predictive numbers in the literature are HIGGS's linear fit above 3 bits and RateQuant's per-quantizer beta calibration.

**Collision risk.** RANKED, MOST DANGEROUS FIRST.

1. RippleKV (arXiv:2608.08684, 9 Aug 2026) — SEVERE, and nine days old. It measures per-layer KV sensitivity by injecting calibrated Gaussian noise into the cache and reading the model's response, explicitly motivated by the claim that existing proxies 'do not measure how perturbations at each layer propagate to the output'. Any framing of the damage law as 'first to measure KV sensitivity by injection rather than derivatives' is dead on arrival. SURVIVING CLAIMS: score-space coordinate (they perturb VALUES and read output KL; the asset perturbs KEYS and reads worst-layer attention-logit correlation), a multi-magnitude calibrated CURVE (they use one alpha), damage denominated in end-to-end perplexity (they use KL), evaluation-at-a-quantizer-configuration to predict that configuration's true damage (they never touch quantizers), the catastrophe gate, held-out bit-width extrapolation, and 13-model breadth. Position the work as 'RippleKV shows response-to-injection beats structural proxies for allocation; we show a calibrated response CURVE is a quantitative predictor of end-to-end damage.'

2. AXELRAM (arXiv:2604.02638, Apr 2026) — SEVERE for the mechanism asset. Same algorithm family as the researcher's own cache (orthogonal transform -> N(0,1/d) -> fixed Lloyd-Max-style codebook), same catastrophe, same culprit model: 'sign pattern sensitivity causes catastrophic PPL spikes (Delta > 50) on certain models (Qwen2.5-3B), while others (LLaMA-3.1-8B) are fully stable', root-caused to 'layer-wise norm heterogeneity'. The Qwen-catastrophic / Llama-immune split is therefore published. What survives: the five-generation Qwen diagnosis (immune -> catastrophic at Qwen2 -> immune by Qwen3.5), the exact decomposition into k_proj bias at boundary layers plus massive activations in middle layers, and the on-demand causal reproduction by injecting a synthetic shared component into an immune model. AXELRAM has none of that and offers a competing explanation that must be explicitly tested and either subsumed or refuted.

3. HeadQ (arXiv:2605.03562, withdrawn) — HIGH on the coordinate. Already known to the researcher, but note it goes further than credited: it does not merely propose score-space, it reports 'Fisher/score-space error predicts attention KL far better than raw key MSE' — so 'score-space beats MSE as a predictor' is claimed, and the pairing of score space with Fisher is claimed. The withdrawal (ethical concerns) weakens it as prior art but does not erase priority in review. Novelty must rest on the calibrated noise->log-damage law, the catastrophic range, and end-to-end perplexity rather than attention KL.

4. HIGGS / linearity theorem (arXiv:2411.17525) — HIGH but bounded, and correctly assessed by the researcher. It owns Gaussian-noise-insertion calibration -> per-model damage prediction, for weights, quadratic, above 3 bits. The overlapping risk the researcher may be underweighting: a reviewer will ask why the KV version is not simply 'HIGGS applied to keys'. The defense is the coordinate change (score space, not storage-space L2) and the regime (x0.99 to x1348, versus a law that visibly diverges below 3 bits).

5. SpectrumKV (arXiv:2606.08635, Jun 2026) — HIGH for the kvcheck.py asset. It already ships a per-model pre-deployment gate for catastrophic INT4 KV failure ('Qwen2.5-7B catastrophically fails under INT4 KV quantization, while Mistral-7B and Gemma-2-9B remain stable') implemented as 'three aggressive NIAH trials'. 'Pre-deployment checker for KV catastrophe' is occupied. The differentiator is that theirs is a trial-and-see probe with no predictive signal, no proxy from the same forward pass, and no calibrated gate threshold — but that differentiator has to be stated, not assumed.

6. Malicious Token Injection (arXiv:2510.17098, Oct 2025) — MODERATE-HIGH. Pre-empts the bare construct: perturb cached KEY vectors with additive Gaussian noise (also zeroing and orthogonal rotations) at controlled

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- HIGGS / linearity theorem (arXiv:2411.17525), author-stated: the validation figure shows the linear approximation holds only for 'relatively higher bitwidths (b > 3.0)' and diverges below — the sub-3-bit and catastrophic regimes are conceded, not solved. Limitations section: 'One direction for improvement is validating the approach across several model architecture types (e.g. Mixture-of-Experts)', plus 'the requirement to use Hadamard transforms for weight incoherence, which may add runtime overheads in some cases.' The entire theorem is weight-side; the KV cache is untouched.
- ReQuant / PQI (arXiv:2503.01901), author-stated: 'neither the gradient-based approximation nor the Hessian-based approximation is accurate' at quantization-scale perturbations, and 'combining the PQI analysis into the quantization process is left as future work.' Also: 'sparse matrix multiplication is slow, and may harm decoding speed during inference', forcing them to 'only adopt minimum sparsity (0.5%)'. Their fix is still a path integral of gradients — a measured perturbation response is a different and cheaper answer they did not try.
- RateQuant (arXiv:2605.06675), author-stated limitations: mixed-precision gains are largest only in a 2.5–4.0-bit sweet spot and shrink at extreme compression or high precision; the method requires an AM/GM sensitivity ratio >= 2.0 to be worth applying; 'Different quantizers exhibit beta values ranging from 3.6-5.3; calibration per-quantizer is essential'; optimal allocations vary by input domain (code vs natural language), 'suggesting future work on context-aware compression'; and evaluation is 'limited to GQA with d_h=128; extension to other attention mechanisms unexamined.' A model-side, quantizer-agnostic damage curve is the direct answer to the per-quantizer-recalibration burden they admit.
- Quantization Dominates Rank Reduction (arXiv:2604.11501), author-stated Limitations verbatim: 'Five models up to 14B; larger models untested. LAMBADA on two models. No wall-clock latency (rank reduction saves attention compute; quantization saves bandwidth). Cache-level quantization validated at INT8/INT4 only.' And: 'We do not claim this extends to all compression settings, all models, or all tasks.' Bit-width coverage beyond INT8/INT4 and model coverage beyond 5 are explicitly open.
- Runtime-Certified Bounded-Error Quantized Attention (arXiv:2605.20868), author-stated: 'The certification is local (per-head, per-step) and does not guarantee end-to-end model correctness.' The authors bound attention-distribution distortion from key quantization and then explicitly decline to connect it to end-to-end damage — the exact bridge the damage law builds.
- RippleKV (arXiv:2608.08684): has NO Limitations or Future Work section, and runs no gradient/Hessian sensitivity baseline. Concretely open by omission: only the VALUE cache is perturbed (keys untouched), only one perturbation magnitude alpha is used (no curve), the response metric is output KL (not score-space, not perplexity), the sensitivity feeds eviction budget allocation only and is never used for quantization bit allocation, and evaluation is LongBench across three model families.
- SAFT (arXiv:2306.03076), author-stated future work: 'techniques for easily identifying the k hyperparameter used in SAFT' — the sensitivity threshold is currently chosen by manual visualization. The general problem of turning a measured sensitivity into a principled decision threshold (i.e., a gate) is admitted open in the one prior work closest to the method.
- GIFT-SW (arXiv:2408.15300), author-stated limitations: results limited to 'LLaMA models' only, quantization limited to 'the GPTQ method', and noise parameters based on 'the QNI approach' with 'other distributions unexplored'. Cross-architecture generality of noise-based salience is admitted unresolved.
- HAWQ-V2 (arXiv:1911.03852), author-stated about its own predecessor: 'HAWQV1 approach only provides relative sensitivity of different layers and therefore requires a manual selection of the mixed-precision setting.' Absolute, calibrated, decision-ready sensitivity remains the acknowledged weak point of the whole Hessian lineage.
- Alignment Collapse Under KV Cache Quantization (arXiv:2606.09864): reports Mistral-7B losing 15.2% of refusals at only 1.03x perplexity, and safety features living in 'a low-dimensional activation subspace 10^2-10^3x more vulnerable to quantization noise'. Open problem stated by construction: perplexity-denominated damage measures are blind to alignment collapse, so any damage law or catastrophe gate defined on perplexity needs an explicit scope statement or a second coordinate.
- Malicious Token Injection (arXiv:2510.17098): authors position cache corruption as 'a reproducible and theoretically grounded threat model for future robustness and security research' — i.e., they explicitly hand off the general robustness question, having covered only GPT-2 and LLaMA-2-7B with adversarial rather than calibration-oriented perturbations.
- AXELRAM (arXiv:2604.02638): attributes catastrophic PPL spikes (Delta > 50, Qwen2.5-3B) to 'layer-wise norm heterogeneity' but verifies this only by showing a gradient-free sign-pattern search removes the spikes — there is no causal intervention isolating the cause, and no predictive test that says in advance which model will spike. The prediction problem is left wide open right next to a phenomenon they document.

### Evaluation, metrics and benchmarks — specifically: published critiques of perplexity as a quality metric, and documented cases where perplexity and downstream task accuracy diverge under model / KV-ca

**State of the art.** There is no single accepted metric; the settled consensus is that perplexity alone is REJECTED for compressed models, and the recommended practice is perplexity PLUS a distance metric PLUS a task battery.

1) Distance metrics are the de-facto SOTA supplement. Dutta et al., "Accuracy is Not All You Need" (NeurIPS 2024) is the standard citation: they propose KL-divergence and %flips and show flips of 12-30% (BnB W8A8/W4A4 on GSM8K) at matched accuracy, and that flips and KL are well correlated. Khanal & Capone (2409.11233) independently propose Jensen-Shannon divergence after finding SparseGPT/Wanda preserve perplexity at 50% sparsity while downstream collapses. Rababah et al. (2607.08734, Jul 2026) push this to "correctness agreement" and pin the safe/degrading/breakdown boundary at Q4_K / Q3_K / Q2_K.

2) For long context, the number that defines the state of the art is LongPPL's correlation: plain perplexity has essentially ZERO correlation with LongBench, while LongPPL (key-token-restricted perplexity) attains Pearson -0.96 with LongBench, -0.90 with LongEval, -0.90 with RULER (arXiv:2410.23771, ICLR 2025). Nothing published beats -0.96 in this territory.

3) For KV cache specifically, evaluation is benchmark-based, not metric-based: Yuan et al. (Findings EMNLP 2024, 10+ methods x 65 settings), SCBench (ICLR 2025, 12 shared-context tasks x 8 LLMs), KVFundaBench (ICML 2026, 5 capability categories + LongGenBench), and the NVIDIA KVPress harness (1175 stars, actively maintained) as the reference implementation. The defining empirical result is KVFundaBench's dichotomy: retrieval tasks stay robust under aggressive compression while high-density reasoning collapses.

4) The best counter-evidence for perplexity-family metrics: "Compression Represents Intelligence Linearly" (COLM 2024) — near-linear correlation between bits-per-character and average benchmark score across 31 independently trained LLMs across 12 benchmarks; and "Give Me BF16 or Give Me Death?" (ACL 2025) — 500,000+ evaluations showing FP8 effectively lossless and INT8 at 1-3% degradation on Llama-3.1. The reconciliation the literature converges on: compression/perplexity tracks capability ACROSS models, but fails to track damage WITHIN a family of variants derived from one base model (LLM-KICK's explicit argument).

**Collision risk.** SEVERE — framing collision on the dependent variable itself. Dutta et al., "Accuracy is Not All You Need" (arXiv:2407.09141, NeurIPS 2024), Section 4.3, already ran a Gaussian-noise-vs-perplexity argument on wikitext-2 and concluded: "perplexity alone is also inadequate in evaluating model compression schemes ... addition of Gaussian noise to the log-probability outputs of the model should maintain the perplexity, while the quality of generation will degrade as the standard deviation of the noise increases (see Table 19)." A damage law whose ground truth is wikitext-2 perplexity damage will be met with this paper on page one of every review. It does NOT collide on method (their noise is in output log-prob space, not cached-key space; they build no predictive curve, no held-out validation, no catastrophe gate), and the asker's measured damage range of x0.99 to x1348 is itself a refutation of the "perplexity is invariant to compression noise" analogy in the KV setting. But this must be confronted explicitly and early, not discovered by a reviewer. Reinforcing this attack: Perplexity Can Miss SAE Feature Damage (2606.03002) shows INT7 and INT6 IMPROVING perplexity while degrading 18.7% / 48.7% of active SAE features; "When Perplexity Lies" (2603.26556) shows a 0.2pp likelihood-space gap becoming a 20.8pp generation gap; and "Perplexity Cannot Always Tell Right from Wrong" (2601.22950, DeepMind) proves perplexity is an unsound model-selection criterion in principle.

HIGH — the cheap-proxy-predicts-KV-damage atlas slot is partly taken. KV-CoRE (arXiv:2602.05929, Feb 2026) is "the first large-scale benchmark of kv-cache compressibility in LLMs": multi-model, five English domains, sixteen languages, gradient-free layer-wise measurement, and it "show[s] that [Normalized Effective Rank] correlates strongly with performance degradation under compression." That is a published cheap-cache-statistic-vs-damage claim at scale, in the KV setting. It does not sink the asker's novelty (rank coordinate not score-space; low-rank compression not quantization; correlation not a calibrated per-model predictive law with a catastrophe gate; no noise-injection calibration), but "first large-scale KV proxy-vs-damage atlas" is no longer available as a claim.

HIGH — the pre-deployment-screen slot has a direct 2026 competitor that also attacks magnitude-based laws. "Fidelity Is Not Safety" (arXiv:2607.28196, 30 Jul 2026) targets the exact acceptance stack kvcheck.py implements ("perplexity within a small factor of the original, downstream accuracy ... inside a confidence interval, and data-free output-fidelity signals"), shows gently-compressed models clear all of it and then fail, and ships a data-free two-axis screen with fixed cross-architecture thresholds. Its central claim is directly hostile: "the governing axis is the coherence of the compression error times its rate; the magnitude of the damage does not predict it." The asker's law is a magnitude-of-score-space-noise law. Mitigations: their result is SVD-low-rank-specific on weights at 7-8B dense scale, they explicitly say they "do not claim it resolves within-quantization subtleties," and KV caches are untouched. But the asker should test whether coherent (structured) key perturbations fall off the noise-response curve — that is exactly the failure mode this paper predicts, and it is cheap to run with the existing perturbation harness. Finding that the curve holds for structured perturbations would be a strong result; finding it does not is a limitation better discovered now.

HIGH — the catastrophic-regime contribution is partly pre-empted. "From Signal Degradation to Computation Collapse" (arXiv:2604.19884, Findings of ACL 2026) already publishes the two-regime picture with a mechanistic diagnosis and shows training-free repair works in one regime and not the other. "The Illusion of Equivalency" (arXiv:2607.08734, Jul 2026) already reports "non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths" and, damagi

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- Dutta et al., Accuracy is Not All You Need (2407.09141), Section 7 Limitations, verbatim: 'Predicting performance degradation of LLMs in the wild is a challenging and open problem and it is possible that any metric calculated on standard benchmarks is insufficient.' They add: 'If the downstream task is very similar to the benchmark on which the quantized model is tested, then accuracy may be sufficient, and distance metrics are not needed'; and 'Flips is only a warning that the behaviour of a model and its compressed version is different -- this may or may not materialize as visible degradation in some downstream tasks.' This is the exact vacancy the asker's calibrated, held-out-validated damage law fills, stated by the field's most-cited critique paper.
- Dutta et al. also leave their own key mechanism unresolved (Section 4.3): 'While it is not clear if adding Gaussian noise to the log-likelihoods is an accurate representation of the behavior of compression schemes, it appears to be a good analogy.' Nobody has verified whether real KV-quantization error behaves like symmetric log-prob noise (which would leave perplexity invariant) or like structured score-space perturbation (which does not). The asker has 22 measured noise curves and 96 quantizer configurations that can settle this directly, and settling it is simultaneously the rebuttal to the paper's attack on perplexity.
- Yuan et al. (2407.01527), 'Limitations and Potential Risks' section, verbatim: 'we only benchmark on models with 10B parameters ... and our tasks are more focused on long input but not long generation, with the latter also being an important, though less mature aspect of long context evaluation due to the open-ended nature of prolonged generation tasks.' They also warn: 'we caution our readers to directly adopt our empirical conclusion without proper evaluation under high-stake scenarios.' The <=10B ceiling is the same hardware ceiling the asker has, and long-GENERATION damage under KV compression remains under-measured.
- KVFundaBench / ShotKV (2502.01941), Section 6 Limitations, verbatim: 'Our scope is token-level retention; complementary axes such as KV quantization and broader capability-preservation analyses can be combined with ShotKV but are out of scope here.' Also: 'The attention-derived score in ShotKV is an effective operational signal for ranking semantic units under a budget; it is not a principled semantic-importance oracle'; and 'extension to fully zero-shot, unstructured long-document settings (e.g., book-scale summarization) remains open'. Their future work: 'while we cover five distinct categories of fundamental abilities, the landscape of LLM capabilities is vast. Future work could extend our framework to assess more specialized domains or multimodal tasks.' Explicit statement that the KV-QUANTIZATION half of the capability-degradation question is unclaimed by the leading KV benchmark.
- The Pitfalls of KV Cache Compression (2510.00231), Section 3 and Conclusion: 'Although the discussion so far generally applies to other eviction policies, the sheer diversity of techniques for eviction means that there is no monolithic explanation for the practical consequences of KV cache compression.' Their conclusion frames their fixes as partial: simple modifications 'greatly lessen degradation, suggesting new directions for new more sophisticated eviction policies.' i.e. no unifying predictive account of KV compression damage exists in their view.
- Fidelity Is Not Safety (2607.28196), Section 7 Limitations: the detector 'thresholds fit six labeled arms plus an 18-point sweep and hold only within the SVD and pruning operator families at 7-8B dense scale; its onset predictions and its one soft edge (extreme pruning) are falsifiable tests, not established operating points, and we do not claim it resolves within-quantization subtleties. We have not tested MoE architectures ... We claim only that the accepted guard stack misses this failure, not that the failure exhausts agentic behavior.' Their own admission that they do NOT resolve within-quantization behaviour, and do not cover KV caches or MoE, is the doorway for the asker's law.
- The Illusion of Equivalency (2607.08734), Conclusion: their layer-sensitivity finding 'motivat[es] future structure-aware precision allocation strategies', and their framing 'motivate[s] behavioral evaluation beyond conventional performance metrics'. They observe non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths but do not predict where a given model's breakpoint lies — precisely the predictive step the asker's per-model curve performs.
- SCBench (2412.10319): 'we identify attention distribution shift issues in long-generation scenarios' — reported as an unresolved phenomenon, and 'sub-O(n) memory methods suffer in multi-turn scenarios'. Any single-forward-pass proxy metric (including worst-layer attention-logit correlation) is implicitly challenged by the multi-turn / cache-reuse lifecycle they argue existing benchmarks neglect.
- LLM-KICK (2310.01382), Conclusion and Limitations: 'We primarily restrict our evaluation to Vicuna (decoder-only architecture) due to its open-source license, high performance, and instruction-following ability. For future work, we aim to investigate how the lost knowledge due to compression can be recovered using parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods.' A single-lineage limitation that the asker's 26-model / 13-lineage atlas directly answers — worth saying so explicitly.
- Paloma (2312.10523) frames the standing open problem for any perplexity-based result: perplexity is normally reported on 'monolithic data held out from training' and one should not assume 'perplexity on one distribution extrapolates to others'; they also find 'loss is dominated by the most frequently occurring strings in the vocabulary'. No published work has checked whether a compression-damage law's coefficients are stable across evaluation domains.
- LongPPL (2410.23771): the cause of PPL's long-context failure is now explained (token averaging drowns key tokens), but the fix requires an auxiliary evaluator model, and the authors discuss its cost in Appendix B.8 rather than eliminating it. A cheap, evaluator-free key-token weighting — or a demonstration that KV-noise damage laws are invariant to the PPL-vs-LongPPL choice — is unclaimed.
- Perplexity Can Miss SAE Feature Damage (2606.03002): explicitly concludes 'behavioral metrics alone are insufficient evidence that full-precision interpretability findings transfer to quantized models, motivating feature-level audits of compression' — i.e. the authors state that a feature-level audit standard does not yet exist. Their construction of matched-perplexity pruning-vs-quantization pairs is an open invitation to build matched-damage-law counterexamples.
- From Signal Degradation to Computation Collapse (2604.19884): 'addressing Computation Collapse requires structural reconstruction rather than mere compensation' — they diagnose but do not predict the onset of the collapse regime, and do not extend the taxonomy to KV cache quantization.

---

### Attention normalization trends — QK-Norm (query/key normalization before the dot product), its adoption timeline across model families, and its effect on activation outliers, attention sinks, and quan

**State of the art.** There are two different "best" here, because the slice splits cleanly.

For measuring what architecture choices actually do: **OlmPool / "Cracks in the Foundation" (arXiv:2608.10296, ICML 2026)** is the state of the art and it is not close. The defining numbers: 26 comparable 7-8B models, 170,000+ GPU hours, data/tokenizer/LR/schedule/extension-recipe all held fixed, 38 released checkpoints per model. HELMET@32K range 29.9 to 56.4 across the pool (26.5-point spread from architecture alone). QK norm is the single largest individual factor — removing Olmo 3's QK-norm + post-sublayer-norm in favor of prenorm gains 6.0 HELMET points; adding them to Llama 3 costs 3.8. Counting how many of the four detrimental features a config has is the single most predictive feature (in-sample R^2 = 0.67, LOO R^2 = 0.61), beating a regression over the individual axes. Attention sinks correlate with better long-context performance at R^2 = 0.38, and QK-norm suppresses both sinks and low entropy. Crucially, none of this is visible from short-context signals: end-of-pretraining loss R^2 = 0.29, best held-out perplexity split (WikiText) R^2 = 0.39, HELMET@8K pre-extension R^2 = 0.32.

For "architectural intervention improves quantizability": the bar is set by **Softpick** (kurtosis 33,510 -> 340, 0% sink rate, quantized models beat softmax under HQQ/BnB/GPTQ with the advantage growing as bits fall, down to 2-bit) and **Outlier-Safe Pre-Training** (excess kurtosis 0.04 vs 1818.56; 35.7 vs 26.5 average over 10 benchmarks at 4-bit; 1.4B on 1T tokens; 2% training overhead). The Qwen team's outlier-driven-rescaling paper (arXiv:2601.22966) adds the in-family number: +2 points average performance and only 1.2 points degradation under W4A4 from absorbing outliers into learnable parameters or gated rescaling.

The number that does NOT exist anywhere: the effect size of QK-Norm on KV-cache quantization damage, measured on models that differ only in QK-Norm. Every source in this slice either asserts it without measurement (OlmPool: 'believed to make models more difficult to quantize'), attributes it post hoc from a deployment run (Intel/HF blog: DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B RULER 0.5291 -> 0.0891 at FP8, blamed on the Qwen2 base lacking QK-norm), or measures a different quantization target (weights/activations, not KV). That gap is the state of the art's shape, and it is the opening.

**Collision risk.** Ranked by how much damage each does to the novelty claim.

**1. SEVERE — the QK-norm-explains-KV-quant-failure claim is already public in prose (2026-08-06, 12 days ago).** The HuggingFace/Intel FP8 KV-cache blog writes: 'a cliff we attribute to its Qwen2-family base lacking QK-norm (so key-cache outlier channels overwhelm the single per-tensor scale).' That is the one-sentence version of the mechanism story. It is a blog, uncontrolled, with no per-layer decomposition, no causal injection, no bit-width sweep, and no damage model — so it does not sink the work — but it does kill the framing 'we discovered that Qwen2's lack of QK-norm is why KV quantization breaks.' Mitigation: cite it in the intro as the practitioner hypothesis, and make your contribution the controlled test plus the quantitative prediction. Do NOT lead with the qualitative claim.

**2. SEVERE (scoop risk, not prior art) — Ai2 built and released the perfect instrument and has the compute to use it.** OlmPool (2608.10296) is 26 released 7B models with 38 checkpoints each, differing only in QK-norm presence/variant, norm ordering, GQA, SWA, and pretrain context. They explicitly wrote that sinks are 'believed to make models more difficult to quantize' and left it there. Any competent group — Ai2 themselves, or anyone who reads that sentence — can run KV quantization over OlmPool and publish 'the first controlled measurement of QK-norm's effect on KV quantizability' in a few GPU-weeks. That paper would subsume the architecture-trend framing of the mechanism asset and would be a strictly stronger design than a 26-model/13-lineage atlas, because it has no lineage confound. This is the single most time-sensitive item in this survey. The counter-move is to do it first: 7B in 4-bit fits a 4090, the checkpoints are on HuggingFace and in a public GCS bucket, and the damage law + score-space pipeline already exists.

**3. HIGH (correctness, not priority) — the Qwen3 report documents the confound that undermines the mechanism narrative.** arXiv:2505.09388 states Qwen3 'remove[s] QKV-bias used in Qwen2 and introduce[s] QK-Norm' in the same sentence. If the mechanism paper says 'QK-Norm made Qwen3 immune,' a reviewer with the tech report open sees a two-variable change attributed to one variable. The k_proj-bias half is confirmed by the same sentence, which is good, but the attribution is not separable within the Qwen lineage. Separating it requires OlmPool (QK-norm varies, no bias change) or a bias-injection ablation on a Qwen3 checkpoint. Flagging this now is cheaper than after review.

**4. HIGH — key mean-removal as a technique is published, twice, recently.** OptR (2608.02691, 2026-08-03) 'first centers the keys before rotation and quantization. This shifts all logits for a query by the same constant and therefore leaves the softmax distribution unchanged,' explicitly to narrow the INT2 quantization range. That is the mean-removal in the production cache, with the same softmax-invariance argument. What survives as novel: the diagnosis (per-head SHARED mean, exact decomposition into k_proj bias at boundary layers plus massive activations in middle layers, causal verification by synthetic injection into an immune model, tracked across five Qwen generations) and the predictive claim (which models need centering and what the damage is if you skip it). The fix itself must be presented as known.

**5. MEDIUM — 'architectural change improves quantizability' is a crowded genre with strong numbers already on the board.** Softpick (kurtosis 33,510 -> 340, wins at 2/3/4/8-bit), OSP (excess kurtosis 0.04 vs 1818.56, 35.7 vs 26.5 at 4-bit), Qiu et al. 2601.22966 (W4A4 degradation only 1.2 points via gated rescaling). None of them is QK-Norm and none of them is the KV cache, so the specific cell is open — but the framing 'we show architecture X changes quantizability' will read as incremental unless the contribution is the PREDICTION (given a model's QK-norm configuration and measured

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- OlmPool / Cracks in the Foundation (2608.10296) states the quantization link as an unverified belief and never tests it: attention sinks are 'theorized to be a result of models attempting to "discard" excess attention weight (Bondarenko et al., 2023; Qiu et al., 2026; Gu et al., 2025) and avoid representation collapse (Barbero et al., 2025); it is also believed to make models more difficult to quantize (Ye et al., 2025).' They then measure long-context performance instead. They have released the 26 controlled models and 38 checkpoints each. This is a stated open question with the instrument already built and public.
- OlmPool admits it could not fully control the comparison: 'Ideally, we would also standardize initialization. However, because not all models have the same parameters (e.g. if GQA or QK norm are added), we cannot exactly synchronize the initializations.' They also note their models are early in pretraining (140B tokens, Chinchilla-optimal) and that the retrieval-head analysis was inconclusive because 'these models may be too weak to reliabl[y]' show the effect. Any downstream user of OlmPool inherits both caveats and can address the second by evaluating a property (quantization damage) that weak models still exhibit.
- OlmPool's headline negative result is itself unexplained at the mechanism level: QK-norm reduces attention sinks and raises entropy, and both correlate with WORSE long context, yet the authors offer only the interpretation that 'attention sinks appear to be the default strategy learned by QK-norm-less transformers to compensate for excess attention.' Why the compensation mechanism matters for retrieval is open, and whether the same mechanism controls quantization sensitivity is untouched.
- Softpick (2504.20966 / Findings ACL 2026) authors state plainly: 'While results at 340M are competitive with softmax, scaling and long-context behavior remain open challenges, motivating future work on softmax alternatives with desirable properties for transformer language models.' They also flag 'an unsolved issue with softpick, in that it returns underscaled scores when used with long context.' And their quantization advantage weakens with scale: 'This becomes less clear at the 1.8B as the gap in performance of the base models is larger. However, the gap does close at lower precisions.' So the sink-removal-improves-quantization result is unconfirmed above 1.8B.
- QK-Normed MLA (2606.16310) authors limit their own claim: 'We do not claim that QK RMSNorm by itself defines an optimization theory, but it gives a direct mechanism for controlling a common failure mode... we treat max-logit statistics as mechanism-level diagnostics.' They document that normalization does not fully bound logits: 'RMSNorm removes the dependence on raw query and key RMS values, but the learned affine weights gamma_q (*) gamma_k can still grow slowly during training,' and observe exactly this sublinear residual growth. They also chose blockwise rather than unified normalization purely for kernel-fusion reasons ('unified QK RMSNorm does not fit as cleanly with the fused, pipelined kernel structure'), leaving the unified variant unablated. Everything is at 400M / 100B tokens.
- Qiu et al. (2601.22966, Qwen team) show outliers are removable but stop at W4A4 weight/activation quantization (1.2 points degradation) and do not evaluate KV-cache quantization at all, despite the fact that their PreAffine/gated-rescaling interventions act on exactly the residual-stream activations that feed k_proj. The KV-cache consequence of removing residual sinks is unmeasured.
- The Spike/Sparse/Sink paper (2603.05498) establishes that normalization configuration can 'suppress massive activations while preserving attention sinks,' i.e. the two are separable — but does not report which of the two, if either, governs quantization error. It identifies pre-norm as the enabling choice without quantifying the quantization consequence of leaving it.
- Wortsman et al. (2309.14322) leave open the more general prediction question they raise themselves: 'One question unresolved by our analysis so far is whether we could have predicted that instability arises when the max attention logit exceeds 1e4 without manipulating learning rate and model size.' Their transplant experiment (passing Q/K through g(z) = sqrt(kappa) * z / sqrt(E[z^2]) for various kappa) is a direct ancestor of controlled score-space perturbation and is explicitly labelled a preliminary step.
- The Intel/HF FP8 KV blog states its two causal attributions as attributions, not findings, and immediately undercuts the simple story: Gemma-3-1B-IT 'does have QK-norm' yet still degrades, which they attribute to the 5:1 local:global ratio rather than key outliers. Neither attribution is tested against a control. Whether QK-norm presence or SWA ratio dominates KV-quant sensitivity is unresolved in the only public document that raises it.
- QUEST (2604.00199) observes that normalizing only Q or only K 'have however, to the best of our knowledge, [not] been proposed in the literature' before their work — meaning the quantization consequences of the key-only-spherical regime (constant-norm cached keys) are entirely unstudied, even though it is the most favorable possible geometry for direction-coding key quantizers.

### Massive activations, attention sinks, and register/sink tokens — the mechanism literature (why these outliers form, what they compute, how to induce/suppress them) and the parallel track where model b

**State of the art.** Three separate "best" answers, because this slice has three sub-questions.

1. BEST MECHANISM ACCOUNT (as of Aug 2026): outlier-driven rescaling. The Qwen Team's unified view (arXiv:2601.22966) is the reference frame — attention sinks and residual sinks both exist so that outliers, acting through their normalizations (softmax, RMSNorm), rescale the non-outlier components; outliers are rescale FACTORS, not contributors. Defining numbers: on a 24B-A3B MoE under W4A4, GatedNorm degrades 1.23 points vs 1.50 for the gated-attention baseline and 2.76 for PreAffine, and on MGSM only GatedNorm stays within 5 points while competitors lose ~10. The complementary refinement is arXiv:2606.02288's Bias Vector Hypothesis: spike-carrying tokens converge after RMSNorm to a rigid constant VECTOR with negligible variance, with W_K contrastively amplifying it, W_Q aligning semantic tokens toward it, and W_V projecting it into the null space. The mechanism is contested at the edges — arXiv:2603.05498 (ICML 2026) says pre-norm makes the two phenomena co-occur and each is independently suppressible at zero LM cost; arXiv:2603.11487 proves sinks are necessary for a class of trigger-conditional tasks; arXiv:2606.20743 shows they rebuild themselves inside a protected residual channel at 160M/290M. There is no consensus, which is a genuine opening.

2. BEST ARCHITECTURAL MITIGATION THAT HAS SHIPPED: head-specific sigmoid gating after SDPA (arXiv:2505.06708, NeurIPS 2025, validated over 30 variants of 15B MoE / 1.7B dense on 3.5T tokens), deployed in Qwen3-Next. Runner-up, philosophically opposite: gpt-oss's learned per-head sink logit (verified in source), which keeps the sink but removes it from token space. Best research-grade alternative: Softpick (ACL Findings 2026), with the sharpest numbers in the slice — 0% sink rate, hidden-state kurtosis 340 vs 33,510, 46.97% attention sparsity, and quantized models beating softmax down to 2-bit — but only at 340M and with an author-admitted long-context bug. arXiv:2602.01203 (ICML 2026) is the paper that shows all three are the same mechanism (implicit vs explicit gating, attention layers as native MoE).

3. BEST CROSS-MODEL MEASUREMENT: arXiv:2605.15572, 27 checkpoints across 8 families under one hooked pipeline. Defining numbers: global activation maxima span nearly four orders of magnitude at comparable parameter counts (Gemma3-27B-it ~7e5 vs Qwen3.5 and MoE at 1e2-1e3); MoE peaks are 14.0-23.4x lower than matched dense; the residual s

**Collision risk.** AGGRESSIVE READ. Ten collisions, ranked by how much damage each does. Three of them are severe and two of them are things you may not know about.

--- SEVERE ---

A. arXiv:2606.02288, "Massive Spikes in LLMs are Bias Vectors" (Chen, Lee, Liou, Verma; Princeton + EnCharge AI; 2026-06-01). VERIFIED via abs page. This is the closest thing to your asset #3 in print. Their Bias Vector Hypothesis states that spike-carrying tokens "converge to constant vectors after normalization," with "negligible variance independent of the input sequence," and they decompose the geometry: "W_K contrastively amplifies the vector, W_Q aligns semantic tokens toward it, and W_V projects it into the spectral null-space." That is "a shared component makes normalized representations collapse onto one direction, and the key projection is what amplifies it" — your mechanism, published in June, with a quantizer (INSERTQUANT) attached. They even handle RoPE, showing the model localizes the bias in "zones of rotational stability" using low-frequency bands and coherent channel pairs. WHAT YOU STILL OWN: per-head KEY-space mean specifically (they work in the residual stream), the exact k_proj-bias-at-boundary-layers plus massive-activations-in-middle-layers decomposition, the five-generation Qwen longitudinal, and causal injection into an immune model. WHAT YOU MUST STOP CLAIMING: that the mean/bias makes normalized vectors collapse to one direction. Read this paper end to end before drafting.

B. arXiv:2605.03562, HeadQ (WITHDRAWN — v3, 19 May 2026, comment verbatim: "Withdrawn by the author because ethical concerns were identified after posting"; v1 5 May and v2 7 May remain on arXiv with full HTML, and are search-indexed). You already flag it, but it is worse than "publishes score-space-as-correct-coordinate." Its HTML says: "Mean-key removal is one simple example of choosing a better representative"; that the token-shared component is "constant across tokens, so it is softmax-null"; that "a perturbation can have large storage error and zero attention damage, while a smaller perturbation in the active direction can change attention sharply"; and it makes three explicit empirical predictions including "key MSE should be a weak behavioral metric" and "score-space error should rank attention damage better." Across six models it reports that Fisher/score-space error predicts attention KL far better than raw key MSE, with same-budget counterexamples, null-space interventions, query-PCA controls and wrong-sign falsification tests. That eats (i) the score-space coordinate of asset #1, (ii) the ENTIRE headline of asset #2 (cosine/MSE uncorrelated with damage), and (iii) part of asset #3 (the token-shared key component is the thing to remove). Withdrawal does NOT clear the field: the abstract and both HTML versions are permanently public and indexed, so a reviewer will find it. Your remaining wedges over HeadQ are end-to-end PERPLEXITY (they measure attention KL and a K-only WikiText-103 decode), the CATASTROPHIC regime, 26 models vs their six, and held-out bit-width extrapolation.

C. github.com/ahb-sjsu/turboquant-pro, docs/KV_KEYS_FINDING.md. VERIFIED by fetching the file. A public, independent, reproducible TurboQuant-based writeup that states your asset #2 headline outright: "PolarQuant K4 has the best key reconstruction (0.095) and the worst perplexity (10643). Per-channel NUQ K3 has 2.4x higher reconstruction error (0.148) and 670x better perplexity (15.8). Cosine-similarity / per-layer attention-output error cannot detect this failure — only a generation (perplexity) metric can." It has a companion theory writeup (github.com/ahb-sjsu/the-angular-observer, "Keep the Angle" v0.8, a deterministic angular-transfer theorem with an explicit condition (A2)), a SHIPPED pre-deployment probe (`turboquant_pro.a2_probe.recommend_key_quantizer`) and a `QualityMonitor` that streams the tangential fraction as a dashboard alert. That is your asset #2 headline AND your asset 

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- arXiv:2605.15572 (Measuring Maximum Activations), Future Work, verbatim: 'Future work could combine intervention experiments, token/feature localization, and end-to-end quantized tasks to explain the mechanisms that create these peaks and determine how controllable they are.' They further concede their INT-8 probe is 'a deployment-relevance sanity check rather than a complete end-to-end quantization evaluation' and that 'end-to-end quantization should be evaluated with richer distributional and task-level measurements.' Their Limitations D.3 opens 'The conclusions of this paper should be interpreted within the boundaries of an observational study' and enumerates eight gaps: English/Chinese/code corpus only; context capped at 4,096 tokens; conflated instruction-tuning stages; INT-8 on only 8 checkpoints with per-tensor recipes; qualitative pattern classification with no quantitative diagnostics; unexplained failures of the Sun massive-activation criterion; missing reproducibility artifacts; and matched comparisons on n=2 (MoE vs dense) and n=3 (base vs instruct). THIS IS THE SINGLE BEST OPENING IN THE SLICE — a published cross-family activation atlas that explicitly asks for the end
- arXiv:2601.22966 (Qwen Team), verbatim: 'we do not investigate why such rescaling is necessary for effective training or representation learning. A deeper theoretical understanding of the role of rescaling remains an open question.' Note what this leaves open and what it does NOT: they have already closed the empirical quantization-robustness question for their own architectural fix.
- arXiv:2504.20966 (Softpick), verbatim from the contributions list: 'We also note an unsolved issue with softpick, in that it returns underscaled scores when used with long context, proposing possible solutions.' Scale limitation is explicit throughout: all results are at 340M parameters. Nobody has yet shown a sink-free softmax replacement holding up at multi-billion scale with long context — which means the pre-2026 architecture your damage law was calibrated on is not going away soon.
- arXiv:2604.14722 (Mechanistic Account of Sinks in GPT-2), verbatim: 'This indicates that attention sinks may arise through distinct circuits across architectures... Consequently, many post-training mitigations may need to be architecture-specific, and many pre-training architectural interventions may not generalize.' They also state the components they identify are 'individually dispensable: architectures omitting each of them robustly exhibit sinks.' This is a strong, citable argument that per-model empirical calibration (your noise-response curve) beats any universal mechanistic rule — use it as your framing.
- arXiv:2606.08105 (Kempner), verbatim: 'Although gated attention improves training dynamics in LLMs, it remains unclear when analogous benefits arise in ViTs'; open questions posed verbatim: 'Can architecture support broadcasting without inducing sink behavior?' and whether attention can be designed to 'preserve what helps learning while removing what hinders it.' Future work should 'test whether they persist or grow with model, data, and training scale.' Two concrete gaps for you: their NOP-vs-broadcast diagnostics (value-norm ratio; stable rank of attention output) were validated only on ViTs (DINOv2-L/G, OpenCLIP-L-16, LeJEPA), NOT on LLMs, and their paper contains no KV-cache or quantization discussion whatsoever. Porting those two diagnostics to LLM KV caches, and showing which sink TYPE predicts quantization damage, is unclaimed and is a 24GB-feasible experiment.
- arXiv:2605.06611 (Structural Origin), verbatim: 'Our empirical validation was conducted on 152M-parameter models. While attention sinks are known to persist at larger scales, confirming the effectiveness of head-wise RMSNorm on models with billions of parameters (e.g., 7B) is a crucial next step.' They also flag that interaction with Mixture-of-Experts architectures is uninvestigated. You cannot pretrain, so head-wise RMSNorm at 7B is out of reach — but their induction protocol at inference time is not.
- arXiv:2604.10098 (the survey), Section 7 Challenges and Future Directions: 'Current AS detection methods assume static sink positions, but sinks can dynamically emerge at non-initial positions.' Also calls for methods to 'Develop efficient and lightweight AS detection methods that maintain inference speed and kernel compatibility,' for 'understanding how AS manifests across emerging architectures,' and notes the absence of standardized evaluation frameworks across model types. The last two are direct invitations for (a) a cross-architecture atlas and (b) a benchmark — but see collision_risk item G, because KVDiagnosis (Aug 2026) is already answering (b).
- arXiv:2508.04257 (KVSink) has no explicit limitations section but states an unexplained observation: 'unusually large norms in the Queries and Values of sink tokens in the final layer, a phenomenon that has not been fully explored in previous research.' Separately their Figure 2 shows sink tokens have SMALLER K/Q/V L2 norms at layer 3. The layer-dependent reversal of sink-token norm behaviour is openly unexplained and is exactly the kind of thing your 22 measured noise curves plus rho measurements on 16 models could settle cheaply.
- arXiv:2603.05498 (ICML 2026) has no labelled limitations or future-work section at all. Its unguarded claim — 'each phenomenon can be independently suppressed without degrading language-modeling performance' — is asserted from Llama2/Llama3/Qwen2.5/Qwen3 probing plus a single 7B/100B-token from-scratch run. Nobody has checked whether the suppressed variants actually quantize better end-to-end at the KV cache. That check is unclaimed, though it needs pretraining you do not have; the read-only version (do models that natively lack sinks show smaller KV damage at matched bit-width?) is within your budget and is the more interesting question anyway.
- Nobody in this slice has a per-model, cheap, forward-pass predictor of KV-quantization damage. The survey asks for lightweight detection; Measuring-Max-Activations asks for the end-to-end link; KVSink and its lineage detect sink tokens but not damage magnitude; the Kempner diagnostics classify sink type but were never connected to compression. The unoccupied intersection is: a per-model damage curve in a score-space coordinate, validated in the catastrophic regime, with an architecture-aware story for WHY the curve differs across families and generations.

Combined with 2603.05498's 'suppressible for free' claim, the field is genuinely split — which means neither side owns the ground, and an empirical damage-prediction result does not have to pick a side.
