### Serving stacks (SGLang, TensorRT-LLM, vLLM, LMDeploy) and what they actually ship for KV-cache quantization, plus the measured throughput/memory/accuracy consequences. Verdict: the slice moved very fa

**State of the art.** Three tiers, with the numbers that define each.

PRODUCTION DEFAULT: FP8 KV (E4M3) in all four stacks (vLLM, SGLang, TensorRT-LLM, LMDeploy). ~2x token capacity, throughput at or near parity when the attention backend fuses dequantization, and typically minimal accuracy loss — SGLang measures Qwen3-235B-A22B aime25 0.7733 -> 0.7333, DeepSeek-R1 0.5067 -> 0.4934, GPT-OSS-120B 0.7533 -> 0.7667 (up). NVIDIA's only published number is 2-3x larger batch on H100 for GPT-J giving ~1.5x performance. The correctness caveat everyone repeats: scaling factors default to 1.0 if absent from the checkpoint, which silently wrecks accuracy.

PRODUCTION-EXPERIMENTAL: 4-bit KV. SGLang ships `nvfp4` and `fp4_mx_block16` at ~3.56x BF16 token capacity (~1.78x over FP8), marked experimental, with NVFP4 prefill kernels still open across SM80/90/100. vLLM merged the TurboQuant backend on 2026-04-15 at 2.6x-4.9x compression and 68-100% of baseline decode throughput. TensorRT-LLM ships NVFP4 KV on Blackwell sm100/103 for a short model whitelist, requiring offline ModelOpt quantization. Accuracy in this tier is wildly model- and benchmark-dependent and unpredicted: same FP4 quantizer, GPT-OSS-120B loses 40 points of AIME25 while DeepSeek-R1 loses 10 and GSM8K loses nothing anywhere; vLLM's TurboQuant 3-bit preset holds NIAH at 100% while GSM8K falls 0.900 -> 0.720.

RESEARCH FRONTIER: 2-bit with serving integration. OSCAR (arXiv:2605.17757) is the number to beat — ~8x KV memory reduction, BF16 accuracy gap of 3.78 / 1.42 points on Qwen3-4B-Thinking / Qwen3-8B, up to 7x throughput at large batch under fixed memory, up to 3x faster batch-1 decode, with a paged-serving-compatible INT2 kernel for SGLang and vLLM. KVarN (arXiv:2606.03458) claims Qwen3-32B AIME25 parity (59.3 vs 59.4) at ~4x capacity with above-FP16 throughput in a native vLLM backend. OptR (arXiv:2608.02691) beats OSCAR by learning per-head rotations against post-W_O output error.

The cross-cutting systems fact: memory savings do not automatically become throughput. SGLang's own docs warn that unfused dequantization can 'negate the memory benefits'; issue #35291 measures FP8 KV costing 43.7% of a decode step on DeepSeek-R1/8xH200 through one bad `.to()`; the SGLang TurboQuant PR loses 25-36% of prefill throughput to rotation overhead; UltraQuant only beats FP8 once context exceeds the FP8 baseline's resident capacity.

And the fact that matters most to you: across every one of these, quality is established by running ben

1. FATAL to asset #4 (the production KV cache) — vLLM PR #38479, merged 2026-04-15. `--kv-cache-dtype turboquant_3bit_nc` is in vLLM today: Walsh-Hadamard rotation + Lloyd-Max scalar quantization on keys, uniform on values, fused Triton store/decode kernels, bit-packed compact slots, CUDAGraph-safe, with a published throughput/quality table on Blackwell. It independently landed the two design decisions you would have claimed — QJL removal ('per community consensus (5+ independent groups found it hurts attention quality by amplifying variance through softmax)') and boundary-layer FP16 protection via `--kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers`. SGLang PR #23135 is a second independent implementation. AMD's UltraQuant (arXiv:2606.20474) publishes the same recipe as a paper. Do not present the WHT+Lloyd-Max+bit-packing cache as a contribution; present it at most as an instrument you used to generate measurements, and check whether your 5.54x is even competitive against the merged presets (2.6x-4.9x) before quoting it.

Move fast; this paper is three weeks old and the natural follow-up is exactly your paper.

This is a reviewer's first question. Run the layer-ablation yourself on your 13 models before you write; if you can show worst-layer beats a sum/mean aggregate out of sample, that becomes a strength rather than a wound.

- WitCert (arXiv:2607.28699), Sec. 6.4.6, verbatim: 'Tightening the universal witness below 8 bits. Until then, selective gating is limited to the 8-bit-class regime and coarser schemes fall back to always-on tiered repair.' This is the single largest opening in the slice for you: the only rigorous gating framework explicitly cannot function in the 2-4 bit regime where your damage law lives, and it names calibration-assisted hybrids as the way out.
- WitCert, Sec. 6.4.6, verbatim: 'Multi-layer composed certificates. Read this as both an opening and a threat — it is an explicit empirical claim that no single layer's pollution is individually sufficient to cause damage, which cuts against a worst-layer coordinate.
- WitCert, Sec. 6.4.6, verbatim: 'Certifying eviction online. The exact distortion of token dropping equals the dropped attention mass and separates methods cleanly offline, but every per-block constant-size witness we tried is vacuous online due to logit cancellation; whether a cheap sound online witness exists for eviction is open.' - TriAxialKV (arXiv:2605.17170), Appendix C Limitations, verbatim: 'The per-tag sensitivity table is calibrated per workload and per model, requiring a one-time offline pass over a small calibration set. ### Frontier and newest open-weight models of 2026 — what attention and KV-cache designs they actually ship, and what that implies for the shelf life of KV-cache-quantization research (specifically: a dam

**State of the art.** Three different "SOTA"s now compete, and the researcher's work sits under all three.

1. ARCHITECTURAL SOTA (the thing eroding the headroom). DeepSeek-V4-Flash: 7% of DeepSeek-V3.2's KV cache and 10% of its single-token FLOPs at 1M context, via CSA+HCA sequence-dimension compression. V4-Pro: 10% and 27%. Runner-up, and far more widely copied: the 3:1 linear-hybrid (Kimi Linear -> Qwen3.5/3.6, Kimi K3), which reduces KV cache by up to 75% and gives 6x decode throughput at 1M. Third: Gemma 4's stack of 5:1 SWA-to-global + cross-layer KV sharing + values=keys, which cuts the global KV cache 37.5% on top of GQA and SWA. Each of these ships the compression BEFORE any quantizer runs.

2. DEPLOYED QUANTIZATION SOTA. FP8, unambiguously, per vLLM's May 2026 independent study: 2x KV capacity, negligible accuracy loss, negligible latency overhead, and better TTFT under burst load — because FP8 quantizes the attention COMPUTATION with hardware tensor cores rather than only the storage. Every TurboQuant variant tested was strictly below BF16 throughput (10–68% latency overhead), and the 3-bit variants lost up to 20 points on AIME25 and LiveCodeBench-v6 and ~30% relative MRCR AUC on Qwen3-30B at 128k–256k. The operating point the researcher's work targets (3–3.5 bits) is currently, in the dominant serving engine's published judgment, "poor candidates for production deployment."

3. RESEARCH-QUANTIZATION SOTA at low bit-width: NOVA-KV (arXiv:2608.04074, two weeks old), which formulates KV quantization as transform coding with attention-product error as the distortion, derives a closed-form NON-orthogonal optimal key transform satisfying a generalized Parseval relation, and applies MSE-optimal vector quantization in the transform domain. At 2 bits per element it recovers most of the long-context retrieval accuracy that scalar methods lose, at comparable throughput, with the widest margin on hybrid-attention MoE models (GPT-OSS-20B, where prior 2-bit transforms collapse at every context length).

The number that defines the shelf-life question, and it is a measured one: on gemma-4-26b-a4b, TurboQuant at 3.5 bits delivers 1.71x total KV reduction — because only 5 of 30 layers are full-attention and quantizable, while 25 are fixed-size sliding-window caches. On a full-attention model the same quantizer delivers roughly 5x. Architecture has already taken about two-thirds of the prize on that model, and Gemma 4 is the most quantization-friendly of the 2026 releases.

**Collision risk.** Be aggressive about all six of these. Two are near-misses that could sink a novelty claim outright.

TIER 1 — DIRECT THREATS TO A CORE ASSET

1. NOVA-KV, "Spend Bits Where Queries Look" (arXiv:2608.04074, 2026-08-04, USC). This is the most dangerous paper in the survey and it is TWO WEEKS OLD. It independently arrives at (a) attention-product/score-space error as the correct distortion criterion — the researcher's coordinate; (b) key mean subtraction as a design element — (k_t − k̄)R_K, i.e. the researcher's per-head-shared-key-mean mechanism, already operationalized; (c) MSE-optimal quantization applied after a transform — Lloyd-Max after rotation; and (d) the architecture-trend framing, with hybrid-attention MoE models as the headline result. Then it goes further and proves the researcher's transform family is suboptimal: "the optimal key transform is not orthogonal... All prior designs used orthogonal transforms, which we show are suboptimal in practice when the goal is to minimize the key-query inner product." A WHT is exactly such an orthogonal transform. The production cache (WHT + Lloyd-Max + residual signs) is now a documented baseline that a stronger, published, open-sourced method beats at 2 bits. What NOVA-KV does NOT have: any predictive damage law, any cross-model atlas, any catastrophic regime, any mechanism diagnosis across model generations. Reposition around those, not around the codec.

2. "Which Decisions Low-Bit Quantization Breaks, and How to Predict Them" (arXiv:2608.06564, 2026-08). Structural collision on the damage law's SHAPE. Same template, same month: measure a cheap per-model quantity, fit a law, validate on held-out conditions, report that constants do not transfer, argue benchmark averages hide the damage. Their multiplicative "margin shrinkage" (c = 0.86 at 4 bits, 0.33 at 3, 0.00 at 2) is the weight-quantization analogue of a noise-response curve; they predict held-out flip rates to a median 1.7 points over 161,744 predictions across 16 models and 8 families. It is weights not KV, decision margins not perplexity, and it stops at 2 bits without a catastrophic regime — so the researcher is not scooped. But the "we found the law that predicts quantization damage" pitch is now occupied, and reviewers will have seen it. Cite it, contrast the setting explicitly, and lead with the two things it lacks: the KV setting and the x1348 catastrophic range.

TIER 2 — OCCUPIES THE NICHE OF A SPECIFIC ASSET

3. WitCert (arXiv:2607.28699, 2026-07-30) vs kvcheck.py + the x5 catastrophe gate. WitCert already delivers pre-deployment/runtime risk gating for KV quantization, but with a SOUND upper bound (Lean 4 machine-checked) rather than an empirical regression, plus live SGLang integration and a demonstrated repair (raw-cast fp8 from 22.8 back to 79.7 on hard RULER). A soundness guarantee beats R^2 = 0.971 rhetorically, even if the bound is looser in practice. Worse: WitCert's headline mechanism finding directly challenges the "worst-layer" coordinate — "in a 28-layer sweep no single layer's pollution alone loses anything (0/28)." A reviewer will ask why worst-layer attention-logit correlation is the right summary if no single layer causes damage in isolation. The researcher's coordinate is a summary statistic of a GLOBAL perturbation, not a single-layer causal claim, but that distinction has to be made explicitly and backed with data.

4. RippleKV (arXiv:2608.08684, 2026-08) vs the noise-response curve. Same primitive: inject calibrated norm-adaptive noise into the cache with NO quantizer involved, measure a divergence at the model output, build a per-model curve. Differences that preserve novelty: values only (not keys), KL at output (not log perplexity damage), per-layer profile (not a scalar-in/scalar-out predictive law), used only for budget ALLOCATION, no held-out bit-width validation, no catastrophe gate, no cross-architecture claim. But "perturbation propagation to the output is the right sensitivity signa

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- MiniMax pre-training lead Haohai Sun, listing what still blocks linear/sparse attention in production: 'Low-Precision State Storage: Linear attention is currently far more sensitive to numerical precision than full attention.' Also on the same list: 'Prefix Caching: In real-world applications, the cache-hit rate for conversations is very high. A new architecture must handle this gracefully' and 'Speculative Decoding: How do you optimize speculative decoding with linear attention backbone?' He adds: 'Well fortunately, all of these seem solvable.' This is the successor problem to KV quantization, stated as open by the lab that hit it, and it is measurable on a 4090.
- The same post, on why evaluation is the real bottleneck: 'We developed proxy metrics for this specific weakness and iterated until the hybrid model seemed to match MHA. But does that proxy metric still correlate with real-world downstream performance at an even larger scale? Are there other hidden weaknesses? Who knows. We haven't run those experiments yet.' And: 'The better the models get, the harder they are to evaluate.' A validated proxy-to-damage law is precisely the missing instrument being described here — by a frontier lab, about their own architecture decisions.
- GLM-5 tech report, on the residual cost of every cache-shrinking architecture: 'all of these methods incur an inherent accuracy gap on fine-grained retrieval tasks—up to 5.69 points on RULER@128K and 7.33 on RepoQA@128K—due to the unavoidable information loss introduced by efficient attention mechanisms during continual-training adaptation, even when half of the layers retain full attention. In contrast, DSA is lossless by construction.' Nobody has measured how a KV quantizer's damage COMPOSES with this pre-existing architectural loss.
- GLM-5 on the DSA indexer, in the RL context: 'the retrieved top-k results are critical for RL stability... storing the indexer's top-k indices at every token position is clearly impractical, since the k=2048 used by the indexer is much larger than the k typically used in MoE.' The corollary nobody has written: quantized keys change which 2048 tokens the indexer selects. That is a discrete selection failure, not a smooth score perturbation, and no damage law in the literature models it.
- vLLM's TurboQuant PR, on the structural blocker for hybrids: 'vLLM requires all KV cache specs in a cache group to have compatible page sizes... TurboQuant uses uint8 slots of 95 bytes (vs bf16 at 256 bytes), and this page size cannot be reconciled with Mamba state cache pages. The fix requires framework-level changes to allow heterogeneous page sizes per cache group — not something fixable from the attention backend alone.' Tested: Qwen3.5-35B-A3B-FP8 fails outright.
- vLLM's TurboQuant blog: 'At the time of writing, TurboQuant supports only models with standard attention mechanisms (e.g. GQA) — models with sliding-window or hybrid attention are not yet supported.' Also, on a length dependence nobody has modelled: 'The degradation is concentrated at the longest context lengths (128k-256k), suggesting that low-bit KV-cache quantization errors accumulate with sequence length.'
- Raschka on DeepSeek V4, flagging the missing evidence: 'Unfortunately, there is no ablation study in the paper' for CSA/HCA. And: 'I would not describe CSA/HCA as better than MLA in a general sense... I would treat CSA/HCA as an efficiency-focused long-context design that appears to preserve modeling quality well in their large flagship model(s) but not necessarily universally better than MLA.' On Gemma 4's PLE: 'we have to take Google's word here that this is an effective and worthwhile design choice. It would be interesting to see some comparison studies.'
- Kimi Linear's discussion, naming its own unexplored direction: 'Despite their distinct approaches to efficient long-context modeling, linear attention and sparse attention are not mutually exclusive. Future work could explore hybrid models that integrate the strengths of both, leveraging the compression and generalization capabilities of linear attention with the fine-grained retrieval advantages of sparse attention.' Kimi K3 ships MXFP4/MXFP8 QAT and notes 'KDA poses new challenges for conventional prefix caching' — an admitted rough edge.
- WitCert's mechanism finding, stated as something benchmarks cannot produce and as the reason per-step bounds are stuck: 'the observable failure of aggressive schemes is cross-layer accumulation, not per-step infidelity—in a 28-layer sweep no single layer's pollution alone loses anything (0/28)—which explains both why aggressive schemes survive and why per-step bounds are intrinsically conservative.' They also concede the certified path 'is stated for non-adaptive queries' and that 'the remaining systems cost is repeated page-in without a request-level repair cache.'
- 'Which Decisions Low-Bit Quantization Breaks' on the non-transferability of fitted constants: 'Borrowed constants are wrong by 18–33 points at 3 bits, so the paired margin set has to be measured per model and bit-width: it locates which decisions break without full generative evaluation, but does not replace measuring.' They also concede 'we treat the 2-bit floor as where the instrument stops measuring' and 'No label-free repair we tested recovers more than one more bit does.' Both the per-model-constants caveat and the missing catastrophic regime are exactly where the researcher's law claims more.
- Alignment Collapse under KV Cache Quantization: 'no universal safe bit width exists,' with 'sharp model-specific phase transitions invisible to standard metrics,' and the damning calibration for any perplexity-based law — 'Mistral-7B loses 15.2% of its refusals at only 1.03× perplexity.' Their PCR diagnostic is validated on nine primary plus one held-out model; generalization beyond that is left open.
- LinearKV, on the architecture-dependence nobody predicted: exact composition of cached recurrent states 'is unnecessary and, on some architectures, even harmful' — it ties with a single-block initializer on two GDN models but collapses on Mamba-2 (46.6% vs 86.8% of full quality under EPIC). They frame their own open question as C2: 'whether one construction stays beneficial across these designs—or must be re-tuned per architecture—is unknown.'
- Rethinking the Role of Efficient Attention in Hybrid Architectures: 'how these efficient modules shape model capabilities remains poorly understood' and 'Existing studies lack a unified mechanistic analysis of how different efficient-attention designs shape the capabilities and training dynamics of hybrid architectures.' Their finding that long-range retrieval is carried by the full-attention layers implies, but does not test, that quantizing those layers is disproportionately dangerous.
- A KL Lens on Quantization, on why hybrids need their own treatment: 'SSMs and hybrid models exhibit different quantization behavior than Transformers: for instance, the state transition in selective SSMs has highly sensitive internal activations, and SSM outputs contain large magnitude outliers not seen in Transformer outputs. These differences call for a more nuanced, layer-wise approach to quantization.' They validate KL over MSE/SQNR for ranking, but do not produce an absolute damage prediction.
- KVDiagnosis states the gap its own single-model scope leaves: 'A label such as "50%" may mean half the positions for one method, fewer key channels for another, and lower numerical precision for a third. A fair benchmark must state what each setting means and use only diagnostics valid for its transformed object.' Their entire benchmark runs on Qwen3-8B — cross-architecture generality is explicitly not established.
- Practitioner-stated, in the merged mlx-vlm TurboQuant PR, and completely unvalidated in any paper: 'the problem is related to having the same rotation matrix to all layers, so errors were accumulating across all layers, that's why when more layers were impacted, the error grew linearly (e1+e2...+e36=36e). If they are pointing to different directions they should almost cancel each other.' Also: 'Skipping just the first and last 2 layers takes PPL from horrible (+243) to (+0.93).' A per-layer independent rotation is a one-line change with an untested, large, and directly measurable claimed effect.

---

### Multi-head Latent Attention (DeepSeek MLA) and its descendants — how much KV compression is now baked into the architecture, and whether post-hoc KV quantization still has anything to eat.

WHAT I VER

**State of the art.** Two separate frontiers, and they do not meet.

**Architectural KV compression (the winner, by a lot).** MLA fixes the cached state at 576 values per token per layer shared across all heads (512 latent + 64 decoupled RoPE) — unchanged since DeepSeek-V2 in May 2024 and identical in every MLA checkpoint I pulled. Headline reductions: 93.3% vs MHA (DeepSeek-V2's own claim), 92.19% via MHA2MLA conversion at 0.5% LongBench cost, 10.6x via X-EcoMLA distillation at <0.1% score drop for 140 GPU-hours. DeepSeek-V4 (Apr 2026) then stacks CSA + HCA + SWA + per-layer 4x/128x compression ratios and lands at **~2% of a BF16 GQA8 baseline at 1M context, and 10% of DeepSeek-V3.2's KV cache**. That 50x is the number to beat, and it is free at inference time because it is in the weights.

**Post-hoc KV quantization ON MLA (barely exists).** The state of the art is FP8, and specifically FP8-on-content-with-BF16-RoPE. SnapMLA (Feb 2026) is the reference implementation — 1.91x decode throughput, near-BF16 quality, and it is FP8 only. DeepSeek-V4 ships the identical mixed-precision policy inside the checkpoint, so the technique is now vendor-default rather than a post-hoc option. vLLM confirms the ceiling in code: every MLA attention backend declares fp8_e4m3 as its lowest supported KV dtype, while non-MLA backends already accept int4/int8/nvfp4/turboquant formats. The only published data point on MLA below 8 bits is MHA2MLA's Table 2, which reaches Int4 on converted models (-96.87% total cache, 25.0 vs 27.4 LongBench) and never attempts Int2 on any MLA configuration.

**The number that defines the opportunity.** On MLA per token per layer: BF16 = 9216 bits; DeepSeek-V4's shipped BF16-RoPE/FP8-content = 5120 bits (1.8x); 3-bit content with RoPE untouched = 2560 bits (3.6x over BF16, only 2.0x over the vendor format) — and at that point the 64 unquantized RoPE dims are **40% of the entire compressed cache**. Quantizing the RoPE block gets you to 1728 bits (5.3x), a further 1.48x. So the whole remaining post-hoc opportunity on MLA is concentrated in a 64-dimensional per-token vector that SnapMLA measured at +/-10^3 dynamic range with outlier tails and an order-of-magnitude FP8 MSE penalty, and that both SnapMLA and DeepSeek deliberately refuse to touch. That is the state of the art and the frontier in one sentence.

**Collision risk.** **Within the MLA slice proper — surprisingly little, but three real hits.**

1. **SnapMLA (2602.10718) owns 'MLA has heterogeneous quantization sensitivity, and the decoupled RoPE block is the fragile part.'** They claim it explicitly as a first: 'we empirically identify, to the best of our knowledge for the first time, the divergent quantization sensitivity between the latent content and decoupled RoPE components in the MLA KV cache.' If any part of your MLA extension is framed as 'we discovered MLA has a sensitive sub-block', that is taken as of Feb 2026. What is NOT taken: measuring it in score space, at bit-widths below 8, or with any damage prediction. Their evidence is a dynamic-range histogram (+/-10^3 vs +/-10^1) and an MSE bar chart.

2. **DeepSeek-V4 (2606.19348) has shipped the mixed-precision-by-role KV policy into the checkpoint.** 'BF16 precision is used for the RoPE dimensions, while FP8 precision is applied to the remaining dimensions.' Any paper proposing 'treat the RoPE dims differently from the content dims in MLA' is now proposing what the frontier model already does by default. Your contribution has to be the bit-allocation *rule* and the damage *prediction*, not the split.

3. **Block-GTQ / RoPE-Aware Bit Allocation (2606.24033, 23 Jun 2026) is the most dangerous paper in this report and you should read it today.** It is built on TurboQuant-MSE (your codebase's algorithm), it does per-RoPE-block label-free energy scoring and greedy integer bit allocation, it evaluates on a *ten-model diagnostic panel*, its fidelity metric is *per-layer MAE on RoPE query-key logits* (a score-space metric on a multi-model panel — the two defining features of your damage law), and its headline result is *catastrophe avoidance* (AIME 51.7/37.5 where uniform TQ-MSE collapses to 0.0/0.0). It also ships a packed-cache serving path at 3.24x. It does not build a calibrated noise→damage curve, does not do noise injection without a quantizer, does not fit an R^2, and is not applied to MLA — but it occupies adjacent ground on four axes simultaneously. If you write 'score-space fidelity on a multi-model panel predicts catastrophic KV quantization failure', a reviewer who knows this paper will ask what is new.

**Adjacent (non-MLA) but aimed straight at your working code.**

4. **vLLM already ships TurboQuant as a first-class KV cache dtype**, with four presets and published numbers in the config docstring: turboquant_k8v4 (2.6x, +1.17% PPL), turboquant_4bit_nc (3.8x, +2.71%), turboquant_k3v4_nc (~3.5x, +10.63%), turboquant_3bit_nc (4.9x, +20.59%). Your 5.54x needs a PPL delta attached or it cannot be compared to 4.9x/+20.59%. Two further barbs in that same docstring: it credits DRIVE/EDEN, HIGGS (arXiv:2411.17525) and AQUA-KV (arXiv:2501.19392, verified real) as prior art for the Hadamard+Lloyd-Max pattern, and it states 'QJL is intentionally omitted: community consensus (5+ independent groups) found it hurts attention quality by amplifying variance through softmax.' If your production cache uses residual signs, you are on the opposite side of a stated upstream consensus and need measurements, not argument.

5. **RotaryQuant (2608.08081, 8 Aug 2026)** — Walsh-Hadamard + block-diagonal SO(4) rotations, 3-bit scalar KV quantization, a fused GPU pipeline running attention directly on packed 3-bit tensors 'without materializing full-precision KV state', targeting 26-120B MoE models on 16-32 GB consumer devices, ΔPPL ≤ +0.0012 and 100% retrieval at 32K. This is your asset #4's system architecture, ten days old, on Metal rather than CUDA. Near-total overlap in shape; check it before claiming novelty on the production cache.

6. **HyperQuant (2606.23406)** claims to beat TurboQuant on KV down to 1.7 bits/scalar using RHT + E8/D4/A2/Z lattices + Rice coding, and includes 'bias-correction methods for the KV cache that keep the reconstruction unbiased under inner products, preserving attention semantics' — i.e. it claims the unbiased-inner-prod

**Open problems (author-stated).**

- SnapMLA, Appendix A (Limitations), verbatim: 'SnapMLA employs FP8 E4M3 quantization for the content portion of the MLA KV cache while preserving the RoPE component at higher precision. Our numerical analyses and benchmark results validate this mixed-precision strategy for the evaluated decoding workloads. Exploring more aggressive precision formats, alternative calibration policies, or substantially longer context lengths remains a promising direction for future investigation.' — The authors who own the MLA-quantization-sensitivity finding explicitly hand off sub-FP8 MLA KV quantization. This is the single most directly claimable opening in the slice.
- MHA2MLA, Limitations, verbatim: 'Considering that MHA2MLA can significantly reduce inference costs, it is worthwhile to validate it on larger and more diverse open-source LLMs. However, constrained by our computation resources... Furthermore, since Deepseek has not yet open-sourced the tensor-parallel inference framework for MLA, it is currently challenging to explore models larger than 7B.' — Their quantization interaction table (Table 2) is ONE model (Llama2-7B), THREE latent widths, and only Int4. There is no Int2 row for any MLA configuration and no second model. A 24 GB GPU can extend that grid substantially without a cluster.
- MHA2MLA's own summary sentence, verbatim: 'has demonstrated its ability to integrate with KV quantization techniques to realize a ~97% cache saving. It is also theoretically compatible with other methods like pruning.' — 'Theoretically compatible' is doing all the work. Nobody has measured whether architectural low-rank compression and post-hoc quantization interact additively, sub-additively, or catastrophically once you push past 4 bits. The prior is sub-additive, because both consume the same redundancy, but it is unmeasured.
- DeepSeek-V4, Conclusion/Limitations, verbatim: 'In pursuit of extreme long-context efficiency, DeepSeek-V4 series adopted a bold architectural design. To minimize risk, we retained many preliminarily validated components and tricks, which, while effective, made the architecture relatively complex. In future iterations, we will carry out more comprehensive and principled investigations to distill the architecture down to its most essential designs, making it more elegant without sacrificing performance.' Also: 'although Anticipatory Routing and SwiGLU Clamping have been proven effective in mitigating training instabilities, their underlying principles remain insufficiently understood.' — The frontier vendor concedes it does not know which of its KV-compression mechanisms are load-bearing. A per-mechanism attribution — how much damage each of CSA / HCA / SWA / FP8-content actually causes — is exactly what a calibrated noise-response curve would deliver, and DeepSeek is publicly asking for it.
- 'Through the Bottleneck' (2607.23054), self-stated: 'We view this as an initial data point and detail scope limitations in Section 5.' The entire mechanistic study is a single 114M-parameter transformer fine-tuned on TinyStories. Their key quantitative claim — 'the bottleneck is globally over-provisioned, using only 46% of its capacity on average' — has never been tested on a real MLA checkpoint. DeepSeek-V2-Lite and Moonlight-16B-A3B both fit on a 4090 in 4-bit and both have the exact same 512+64 geometry.
- EG-MLA (2509.16686), verbatim: 'While MLA already achieves significant KV cache reduction, the scope for further compression remains limited without performance loss.' — an explicit statement from an MLA-extension paper that the architectural well is running dry. That is the strongest available argument that post-hoc compression on MLA still matters, coming from a party with no incentive to say it.
- 'Beyond KV Reconstruction' (2607.27269), verbatim: 'low-rank factorization and RoPE handling introduce attention-function errors that may be tolerable for standalone generation but substantially lower draft-token acceptance.' — In-slice, published evidence that end-to-end generation metrics UNDER-DETECT MLA compression damage. They fix it with a functional-reconstruction objective, but they never build a predictive damage model — they measure 192 configurations empirically and report cell-by-cell. The predictor is missing.
- CARE (2603.17946), on the whole prior line of MLA conversion work: baselines 'focus on minimizing the difference between weight matrices rather than on how those weights affect input activations, ignore the covariance structure of activations, and enforce uniform rank across layers, causing activation drift and degraded attention fidelity.' — They fix the coordinate at the ACTIVATION level (activation-preserving factorization) but stop short of the SCORE level. The step from activation-space fidelity to attention-logit-space fidelity is the one they left on the table.
- TPLA (2508.15881) applies Hadamard/PCA transforms to the MLA latent purely to reduce cross-shard interference and reports 'minimal accuracy degradation' — but never asks what those same transforms do for quantizability of the latent. The rotation infrastructure exists in-slice and its quantization use is unclaimed.
- GQLA (2605.15250) and Multi-Head Low-Rank Attention (2603.02188) both establish that MLA exposes multiple algebraically equivalent decode paths (MQA-absorb vs expanded per-group/per-head). No one has asked whether a quantizer applied on the absorbed path (quantize c_KV) and one applied on the expanded path (quantize reconstructed per-head K/V) produce the same damage. They cannot: quantizing a 512-dim shared latent injects a rank-1-in-head-space error that every head sees through its own W_UK, whereas quantizing reconstructed keys injects independent per-head errors. Nobody has measured the amplification factor.
- vLLM ships no sub-8-bit KV dtype for ANY MLA backend (verified in vllm/v1/attention/backends/mla/*.py at main). RFC #51751 / PR #52670 just landed a KVCacheDTypeHandler registration protocol precisely so third parties can add formats without editing core. That is a stated, open, dated integration point with no MLA claimant.
- A Random Matrix Theory Perspective on MLA (2507.09394) shows that decoupled-RoPE MLA 'prevents this cascade, maintaining broad spectral support and suppressing outlier formation across layers' — but this is measured on small pretraining runs with MP diagnostics only, never connected to quantization robustness, and never validated on a released MLA checkpoint. The prediction 'MLA models are constitutionally immune to normalized-key collapse' is stated implicitly and tested nowhere.

**Key works.**

- `arXiv:2405.04434 (v5, 19 Jun 2024)` (2024-05-07) **DeepSeek-V2: A Strong, Economical, and Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Language Model** — Origin of MLA. Compresses K and V jointly into a 512-dim latent plus a 64-dim decoupled RoPE vector shared across all heads; claims 93.3% KV cache reduction vs DeepSeek-67B and 5.76x max generation throughput. The kv_lora_rank=512 / qk_rope_head_dim=64 geometry set here is verbatim unchanged in ever *Relevance:* This is the baseline your quantizer would sit on top of. The decoupled-RoPE split is the structural fact that governs everything downstream: 512 content dims that behave like a well-conditioned latent, 64 RoPE dims that do not.
- `arXiv:2606.19348 (26 Apr 2026)` (2026-04-26) **DeepSeek-V4: Towards Highly Efficient Million-Token Context Intelligence** — 1.6T/49B-active Pro and 284B/13B-active Flash, 1M context. Hybrid attention combining Compressed Sparse Attention (CSA, with a lightning indexer, index_topk=512) and Heavily Compressed Attention (HCA), plus SWA(128) and attention sinks. Per-layer compress_ratios alternate 4x / 128x (read from the re *Relevance:* This is the single most important paper in the slice for you. It is the vendor absorbing your product category: low-rank latent, sparsity, sliding window, AND mixed-precision-by-role KV storage are all now inside the checkpoint. Your post-hoc quantizer no longer starts from BF16 — it starts from FP8
- `arXiv:2602.10718 (v3, 28 Apr 2026)` (2026-02-11) **SnapMLA: Efficient Long-Context MLA Decoding via Hardware-Aware FP8 Quantized Pipelining** — Meituan LongCat + Tsinghua. First open-source FP8 decoding framework for MLA. Their measured finding (§3.1.1, Figure 3): on LongCat-Flash-Thinking the decoupled RoPE component spans +/-10^3 with distinct outlier tails while the content latent is concentrated within +/-10^1; FP8 causes 'an order-of-m *Relevance:* The paper that already owns 'MLA has heterogeneous quantization sensitivity, RoPE is the fragile part' — they explicitly claim it as first. But they stop at FP8 and their own Limitations appendix hands you the next step. Their +/-10^3 vs +/-10^1 dynamic-range split is a direct MLA analogue of your m
- `arXiv:2502.14837 (v2, 3 Oct 2025; ACL 2025)` (2025-02-20) **Towards Economical Inference: Enabling DeepSeek's Multi-Head Latent Attention in Any Transformer-based LLMs (MHA2MLA)** — Converts a pretrained MHA/GQA model to MLA with 0.3-0.6% of pretraining data, via partial-RoPE removal plus joint SVD of K and V. Llama2-7B KV cache -92.19% at 0.5% LongBench drop. Critically, Table 2 measures MLA x post-hoc KV quantization directly: Llama2-7B BF16 = 27.4 LongBench avg; Int4 HQQ (-7 *Relevance:* The ONLY published measured grid of MLA x post-hoc quantization I could find, and it is a 4-cell grid on one model. Two things jump out: (a) MLA + Int4 at 96.87% reduction beats Int2 alone at 87.5% by 3.8 LongBench points — architecture + moderate quantization strictly dominates aggressive quantizat
- `arXiv:2502.07864 (v5, 12 Jun 2025)` (2025-02-11) **TransMLA: Multi-Head Latent Attention Is All You Need** — Converts any GQA model to MLA with DeepSeek-codebase compatibility (vLLM/SGLang). 93% KV compression on LLaMA-2-7B, 10.6x inference speedup at 8K, ~6B tokens of fine-tuning to recover. Explicitly frames combining with 'DeepSeek's advanced features, such as FP8 quantization and Multi-Token Prediction *Relevance:* The other conversion route to get an MLA model onto a 4090. Note that both TransMLA and MHA2MLA gesture at quantization compatibility without measuring it below 4 bits — the composition is asserted, not characterized.
- `arXiv:2503.11132 (v4, 8 Sep 2025)` (2025-03-14) **X-EcoMLA: Upcycling Pre-Trained Attention into MLA for Efficient and Extreme KV Compression** — Post-training distillation ('dark knowledge' of a well-trained teacher) to upcycle pretrained attention into a hybrid MLA variant. Llama3.2-1B-Instruct: 6.4x KV compression at equal average score with 3.6B tokens / 70 GPU-hours on MI300; 10.6x compression at <0.1% average drop with 7B tokens / 140 G *Relevance:* Establishes the compression-vs-training-budget frontier for architectural KV compression: ~10x costs 140 GPU-hours of distillation. - `arXiv:2603.17946 (18 Mar 2026; ICLR 2026)` (2026-03-18) **CARE: Covariance-Aware and Rank-Enhanced Decomposition for Enabling Multi-Head Latent Attention** — GQA->MLA conversion at fixed KV width. Argues explicitly that SVD-style conversion baselines 'focus on minimizing the difference between weight matrices rather than on how those weights affect input activations, ignore the covariance structure of activations, and enforce uniform rank across layers,  *Relevance:* Independent, ICLR-accepted confirmation of your central methodological thesis — reconstruction error in the wrong coordinate is uncorrelated with real damage — arriving from the architecture-conversion side rather than the quantization side. Not MLA, but it is your codebase's algorithm (TurboQuant-MSE), your diagnostic style (multi-model panel), a logit-space fidelity metric (per-layer MAE on RoPE QK logits), and your headline application (catastrophe avoidance: 51.7 vs 0.0). - `arXiv:2505.21487 (27 May 2025)` (2025-05-27) **Hardware-Efficient Attention for Fast Decoding (GTA / Grouped Latent Attention)** — Two MLA-adjacent designs. ## Maintainer's reading — what this changes

*(written 2026-08-18 by the session that ran the survey; the numbered claims above
are agent output, this section is judgement.)*

### Claims that no longer survive, and must be dropped or reframed

1. **The codec.** vLLM merged `--kv-cache-dtype turboquant_3bit_nc` on 2026-04-15
   with the same components (WHT rotation + Lloyd-Max keys, uniform values,
   bit-packed slots, fused Triton kernels) **and the same name**. llama.cpp made
   Hadamard KV rotation the silent default on 2026-04-01. Nothing about this
   repository's quantizer is a contribution any more, and the project name
   collides with an upstream feature.
2. **Key mean subtraction.** Published with the identical softmax
   shift-invariance argument by SageAttention (ICLR 2025), NSNQuant, and OptR.
   §2.3 of the manuscript already concedes NSNQuant; SageAttention is earlier
   still and is not yet cited.
3. **The score-space coordinate.** 2604.11501 measures damage in the softmax
   Fisher metric; HeadQ published the same qualitative claim.
4. **Noise-injection sensitivity profiling.** RippleKV (2608.08684) injects
   calibrated perturbations with no quantizer and reads a per-model sensitivity
   profile — the damage law's mechanism, published nine days before this survey.
5. **"vLLM cannot represent a per-head key offset."** False since
   `int4_per_token_head` merged with a real asymmetric zero-point.

### What still looks defensible

- The **catastrophic regime**. HIGGS's authors state their model "diverges on
  lower bitwidths" (b < 3.0); 2604.11501 validated "at INT8/INT4 only"; WitCert
  concedes its gating is "vacuous for 2-4 bit schemes". Our subject is 2-3 bits
  at x100-x1400 damage.
- A **multi-magnitude calibrated curve** giving an absolute predicted multiplier,
  against RippleKV's single perturbation magnitude and relative ranking.
- **Keys-and-logits** rather than values-and-output-KL.

### The two experiments worth doing next

1. **Is per-layer damage additive?** NVIDIA ModelOpt #2211 and HIGGS both
   allocate bits assuming additivity. Our coordinate is worst-layer — a max.
   If damage is max-dominated, additive allocators mis-spend bits systematically.
   **Testable by re-analysis of the existing 96-configuration dataset; no new GPU
   time.** A clean negative (additivity fits fine) is also worth reporting.
2. **OlmPool.** Ai2 released 26 pretrained 7-8B models varying only QK-Norm, norm
   ordering, KV-head count, SWA and context length, and explicitly declined to
   measure quantization. This repairs a real hole in our own work: the Qwen3 tech
   report states Qwen3 removed the QKV bias **and** added QK-Norm in the same
   release, so the lineage story in SS6.20 is confounded at the vendor source.
   Pre-registerable prediction: QK-Norm is RMSNorm on head_dim — it rescales but
   does not center — so it should collapse per-token norm variance while leaving
   rho untouched, and therefore should NOT improve KV quantizability.

### A threat to our own ground truth

LongPPL (2410.23771) measures standard perplexity against LongBench at r = -0.11.
The damage law is calibrated on wikitext-2 perplexity. This belongs in the
limitations section before a reviewer puts it there.

### An unclaimed, zero-kernel opening

vLLM issue #40069 has two boxes unchecked since April: a per-layer sensitivity
sweep to inform `--kv-cache-dtype-skip-layers` defaults, and a published
quality/compression/throughput config table. The actuator is merged and takes
bare layer indices. Our atlas lacks the throughput axis; everything else exists.
